                                        Directed Isoperimetry and Monotonicity Testing: A Dynamical
                                                                  Approach
                                                                             Renato Ferreira Pinto Jr.∗
                                                                               University of Waterloo
                                                                          renato.ferreira@uwaterloo.ca
arXiv:2404.17882v2 [cs.DS] 1 Oct 2024




                                                                                          Abstract

                                                  This paper explores the connection between classical isoperimetric inequalities, their directed
                                              analogues, and monotonicity testing. We study the setting of real-valued functions f : [0, 1]d →
                                              R on the solid unit cube, where the goal is to test with respect to the Lp distance. Our goals
                                              are twofold: to further understand the relationship between classical and directed isoperimetry,
                                              and to give a monotonicity tester with sublinear query complexity in this setting.
                                                  Our main
                                                         √ results are 1) an L2 monotonicity tester for M -Lipschitz functions with query com-
                                              plexity e dM 2 /ε2 ) and, behind this result, 2) the directed Poincaré inequality distmono (f )2 ≤
                                                    O(
                                                      − 2
                                                                                                       −
                                                                                                                                     2
                                              C E |∇ f | , where the “directed gradient” operator ∇ measures the local violations of mono-
                                              tonicity of f .
                                                  To prove the second result, we introduce a partial differential equation (PDE), the directed
                                              heat equation, which takes a one-dimensional function f into a monotone function f ∗ over time
                                              and enjoys many desirable analytic properties. We obtain the directed Poincaré inequality by
                                              combining convergence aspects of this PDE with the theory of optimal transport. Crucially
                                              for our conceptual motivation, this proof is in complete analogy with the mathematical physics
                                              perspective on the classical Poincaré inequality, namely as characterizing the convergence of the
                                              standard heat equation toward equilibrium.

1     Introduction
One of the central problems in the field of property testing is monotonicity testing: given a function
f defined over some partially ordered domain, decide whether f is monotone, i. e. f (x) ≤ f (y)
whenever x ⪯ y, or ε-far from any monotone function under a given distance metric. Since the
introduction of this problem [GGLRS00] and especially over the last decade, a series of works has
revealed striking connections between monotonicity testing and directed analogues of well-studied
and ubiquitous isoperimetric inequalities such as Poincaré, Margulis, and Talagrand inequalities.
    Let p, q ≥ 1. Following the notation of [Fer23], we say a (classical) (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality
is an inequality of the form
                                     distconst (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇f ∥pq
                                                                  
                                         p

for, say, all functions f : {0, 1}d → R on the Boolean cube, or all functions f : [0, 1]d → R on
the unit cube, or perhaps only the Boolean-valued functions on these domains. Here distconst
                                                                                          p  (f )
                p
denotes the L distance of f to the closest constant function when the domain is given uniform
probability measure. For example,

    1. for functions f : {0, 1}d → R, the classical Poincaré inequality Var [f ] ≤ C Inf [f ], where
       Inf [f ] denotes the total influence of f , is an (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality (see e. g. [O’D14]);

    2. in the same setting, the Talagrand inequality [Tal93] is an (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality;

    3. for smooth functions f : [0, 1]d → R, the (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality is often called the Poincaré
       inequality, especially in mathematical analysis (see e. g. [BGL14]); and

    4. in the same setting, the (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality was proved by Bobkov & Houdré [BH97].

    A series of works on monotonicity testing has shown that many of these inequalities enjoy
natural “directed analogues”, as identified by [CS16]. Let distmono
                                                                 p  (f ) denote the Lp distance of f
to the closest monotone function. Then a directed (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality is an inequality of
the form
                                   distmono (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥pq ,
                                                                
                                       p

where ∇− f := min{0, ∇f }, the directed gradient of f , captures the local violations of monotonicity1.
For example (listing by type of inequality rather than in historical order),

    1. an (L1 , ℓ1 ) inequality was proved by [GGLRS00] for functions2 f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1};

    2. [Fer23] gave the same inequality for Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R;

    3. [KMS18] proved the stronger (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality for functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}, in analogy
       with Talagrand’s result in the classical setting; and

    4. related directed isoperimetric statements, not in Poincaré form, include a directed analogue of
       the Margulis inequality by [CS16], and isoperimetric inequalities for real-valued functions on
       the Boolean cube [BKR24] and for Boolean functions on the hypergrid [BCS23; BKKM23].
   1
     Note that both the classical and directed inequalities relate a local property (violations of the “constant” or
“monotone” property, captured by the gradient) to a global one (distance to a constant or monotone function).
   2
     For the purposes of exposition, we limit the present discussion to the Boolean cube and unit cube domains; in
particular, we do not extend the notation and discussion to hypergrid domains.




                                                         1
    What makes the story above especially compelling is that each new directed isoperimetric
inequality also enabled an algorithmic result, namely a monotonicity tester with improved query
complexity in the same setting. While we refrain from a full review and refer to [Bla23] instead,
one central example is that, for functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}, the celebrated
                                                                        √ 2      work of [KMS18]
essentially resolved the question for nonadaptive testers by giving a O( d/ε ) query tester.
                                                                      e
    In this paper, we seek to further understand the connection between classical and directed
isoperimetry, and the role of the latter in monotonicity testing. Building upon [Fer23], we pursue
this goal by studying the fully continuous setting, namely functions f : [0, 1]d → R. Let us offer
two reasons for this choice, which we view as connections along two conceptual “axes”:

       Classical versus directed. The continuous setting is central to the study of isoperimetric
       phenomena. Ever since the original “isoperimetric problem” about shapes in Euclidean space,
       isoperimetric inequalities have enjoyed a rich and fruitful history with connections to mathe-
       matical physics, geometry, probability theory, diffusion processes, optimal transport, and so on;
       and closest to our subject, the Poincaré inequality itself first appeared in the study of partial
       differential equations arising in mathematical physics [Poi90]. Thus, if there are unifying prin-
       ciples underlying both classical and directed isoperimetric phenomena, it seems reasonable to
       expect such a principle to manifest itself in the continuous setting.

       Discrete versus continuous. Phenomena involving functions on Boolean and continuous do-
       mains are often intimately related3, which we may interpret as a form of “robustness” of the
       phenomena. Thus, it is natural to ask about the full scope of this connection in the case of
       directed isoperimetry and monotonicity testing. While [Fer23] started to answer this question
       by giving an L1 monotonicity tester with O(d) query complexity via a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré
       inequality,
          √        many questions remain. For example, they left open the possibility of a tester with
       O( d) query complexity, which would bring the continuous landscape closer to the discrete one.

   Our main result in this paper is a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality for sufficiently regular
functions f : [0, 1]d → R (in particular, Lipschitz continuity suffices; see Section 5.1):

Theorem 1.1 (Directed Poincaré inequality). There exists a universal constant C > 0 such that,
for all f ∈ H 1 ((0, 1)d ),
                              distmono (f )2 ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥22 .
                                                           
                                  2                                                         (1)

    We highlight two related aspects of this result. First, it takes the same form as the most
classical form of the Poincaré inequality, namely the (L2 , ℓ2 ) form. In contrast, [Fer23] gave an
(L1 , ℓ1 ) inequality, which does not have a natural classical counterpart4.
    Second, the main theme of our proof of this result is the study of the convergence of a partial
differential equation (PDE), which is the original motivating problem for the Poincaré inequality.
The idea is to take one of the central properties of the Poincaré inequality—that it characterizes
the convergence of the heat equation to equilibrium—and modify that PDE in a natural way so that
it converges to a monotone (rather than constant) equilibrium, then derive from its (exponential)
convergence the directed inequality. In fact, plugging in the unmodified heat equation into our
proof recovers a long but unsurprising proof of the classical Poincaré inequality. We contend that,
in the continuous setting, exponential convergence of a PDE to its constant/monotone equilibrium
is the unifying principle behind the classical and directed inequalities.
   3
   See e. g. [O’D14, Chapter 11] and recent works such as [KOW16; CHHL19; DNS21; AHLVXY23; EMR23].
   4
   In the classical settings, as noted above, we have the stronger (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality instead. Note that the ℓ2
(Euclidean) norm enjoys many nice properties that the ℓ1 norm does not (e. g. rotation invariance, being self-dual).


                                                         2
    As an application, we obtain a monotonicity
                                         √      tester for Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R, with
respect to the L2 distance, using roughly d queries:

Theorem 1.2. There exists a nonadaptive, directional derivative
                                                           √    L2 monotonicity tester for M -
Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1] → R with query complexity O( dM /ε2 ) and one-sided error.
                              d                          e    2


    This result answers affirmatively (up to a logarithmic factor) the question asked by [Fer23].
We remark that the algorithm above is also an Lp tester for any p ∈ [1, 2], that the directional
derivative queries may be replaced by value queries as long as f is sufficiently smooth, and that
the dependence on d is optimal among a natural generalization of pair testers to the continuous
setting; see the next section for definitions and details.
    The rest of the introduction proceeds as follows. In Section 1.1, we define our testing model and
describe our monotonicity tester; in Section 1.2 we give an overview of our proof of the directed
Poincaré inequality; and in Section 1.3, we discuss our results and some open questions.

1.1   Monotonicity testing of Lipschitz functions
In this section, let Ω := [0, 1]d for simplicity of notation. For a function f ∈ Lp (Ω) and p ≥ 1, we
define the Lp distance to monotonicity of f as

                      distmono (f ) := inf ∥f − g∥Lp (Ω) : g ∈ Lp (Ω) monotone ,
                                          
                          p

                                      1/p
where ∥f − g∥Lp (Ω) = Ω (f − g)p dx
                        R
                                           by definition.
    As announced, our main algorithmic result is a monotonicity tester with respect to the L2
distance, so we adopt the Lp testing model of [BRY14]. The specific problem we consider—Lp
testing monotonicity of Lipschitz functions—is the same as [Fer23]. Let us formally define the
model. We say that function f is M -Lipschitz if |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M |x − y| for all pairs of points
x, y. Then, the testing model is as follows:

Definition 1.3 (Monotonicity tester). Let p ≥ 1. Given parameters d ∈ N and M, ε > 0, we
say a randomized algorithm A is an Lp monotonicity tester for M -Lipschitz functions with query
complexity m(d, M, ε) if, for every M -Lipschitz input function f : [0, 1]d → R, algorithm A makes
m(d, M, ε) oracle queries to f and 1) accepts with probability at least 2/3 if f is monotone; 2) rejects
with probability at least 2/3 if distmono
                                     p    (f ) ≥ ε.

   As usual, an algorithm is nonadaptive if it decides all its queries in advance before seeing any
output from the oracle, and it has one-sided error if it accepts monotone functions with probability
1. As in [Fer23], we allow two types of oracles queries:

   Value query: Given point x ∈ Ω, the oracle outputs the value f (x).

   Directional derivative query: Given point x ∈ Ω and direction v ∈ Rd , the oracle outputs
   the directional derivative ∇v f (x) = v · ∇f (x), or the symbol ⊥ if f is not differentiable at x.

   We remark that Lipschitz functions on Ω are differentiable almost everywhere (i. e. outside a set
of measure zero) by Rademacher’s theorem, so non-differentiability is not a concern, and they are
bounded and hence in Lp (Ω) for any p ≥ 1, so distmono
                                                  p    (f ) is always well-defined.




                                                   3
The algorithm. Our tester may be seen as the natural continuous analogue of the path tester of
[KMS18] for the Boolean cube, which samples points x ⪯ y ∈ {0, 1}d connected by a path of length
2k , for k sampled uniformly from [log d], and rejects if f (x) > f (y). In our case, a path is replaced
by a direction v ∈ {0, 1}d , and the condition f (x) > f (y) is replaced by the condition v · ∇f (x) < 0,
for a uniformly random point x ∈ [0, 1]d , via a directional derivative query. Such a tester accepts
any monotone function, so the challenge is to ensure that it detects the   case  when  distmono

at least one entry i from ∇f (x) satisfying (∇f (x))i < −τ (this idea allows us to “forget” about
all the other negative entries of ∇f (x), and focus on a simpler “good event”). By ranging over all
possible τ , we show that there exists τ and corresponding p such that, with good probability, the
contribution of the positive entries of ∇f (x) to v · ∇f (x) is smaller than τ , so that v · ∇f (x) < 0.

Remarks on the tester. As observed in [BRY14], for 1 ≤ p ≤ q and any (say) Lipschitz f and
g, Jensen’s inequality gives ∥f − g∥Lp (Ω) ≤ ∥f − g∥Lq (Ω) . Thus any Lq monotonicity tester is also

an Lp tester, so that the O(
    A second remark is that the directional derivative queries, while convenient for the analysis and
in our opinion conceptually clean, can be replaced with value queries under reasonable assumptions.
First, in Remark 3.3 we note that the rejection condition v · ∇f (x) < 0 may in fact be replaced with
the more robust condition v · ∇f (x) < −Θ(ε/d). Now, suppose the input function f is promised to
be twice-differentiable with second derivatives bounded by any constant β. We may then replace
any directional derivative query v · ∇f (x) with the value queries f (x) and f (y), where y = x + αv
for sufficiently small α > 0 as a function of β, ε and d, and reject if f (y) < f (x). This is possible
because, if v · ∇f (x) < −Θ(ε/d) and for such small α, the directional derivative v · ∇f (z) remains
negative on all points z in the line segment between x and y, and hence f (y) < f (x). Conceptually,
we view this as √ confirmation that directional derivative queries are not unreasonably powerful.
    Finally, the d dependence in the query complexity is optimal for derivative-pair testers, which
are randomized nonadaptive testers that, at each step, either sample points x ⪯ y and use value
queries to reject if f (x) > f (y), or sample point x and direction v ⪰ 0 and use a directional
derivative query to reject if v · ∇f (x) < 0. The proof is not difficult, and is given in Section 4.

1.2   Proof overview
Our guiding ideal is to prove Theorem 1.1 by identifying a robust approach to the classical Poincaré
inequality such that, by “toggling” a single aspect of the approach, we can transform a proof of the
classical statement into a proof of its directed counterpart.




                                                    4
1.2.1   Starting point: the heat equation
The first step is to identify the right classical starting point. For example, the Poincaré inequality
can be proved using Fourier analysis, but since the directed problem is highly nonlinear (due to
the ∇− operator), this approach does not seem suitable. Instead, we take a physically-motivated
approach. Let u = u(t, x) where we think of the first variable as time, with each f = u(t) = u(t, ·) a
function over space. A remarkable property (see e. g. [BGL14, Chapter 4]) of the Poincaré inequality

                                         Var [f ] ≤ C E ∥∇f ∥22
                                                               

(where we have written Var [f ] in the place of distconst
                                                    2     (f )2 , which is the same thing) is that it is
equivalent to exponential decay of variance in the heat equation

                                              ∂t u = ∆u ,                                           (2)

where ∆ denotes the Laplacian operator, given appropriate “no-flux” boundary conditions which
we do not Rexpand on atR the moment. To see this equivalence, assume for simplicity the mean zero
condition u(0) dx = u(0, x) dx = 0 (which is then preserved over time), compute ∂t Var [u(t)],
differentiate under the integral, apply (2) and integrate by parts to obtain
                     Z              Z                   Z                    Z
                           2
   ∂t Var [u(t)] = ∂t u(t) dx = 2 u(t)∂t u(t) dx = 2 u(t)∆u(t) dx = −2 ∇u(t) · ∇u(t) dx .

This means that ∂t Var [u(t)] ≤ −C Var [u(t)] if and only if Var [u(t)] ≤ C2 ∥∇u(t)∥22 dx, i. e. expo-
                                                                            R

nential decay of the variance is equivalent to the Poincaré inequality.
   We observe that solutions to the heat equation converge to a constant equilibrium (taking the
average value of u), and the associated Poincaré inequality is a bound on the distance to a constant
function. Accordingly, it seems intuitive that if we replace (2) with a PDE that converges to a
monotone function, we might learn something about the distance to a monotone function instead.

1.2.2   The directed heat equation
One challenge we face is that analyzing directed analogues of (2) in the multidimensional setting
proves challenging due to the interaction between nonlinearities in multiple dimensions. Hence, let
us focus on the one-dimensional case for now. In one dimension, the heat equation is

                                            ∂t u = ∂x ∂x u .

The surface reading of this PDE is that, if we focus on the value of u at a single point x, the
PDE tells us how this value changes over time. Another useful perspective is to think of u(t, x)
as the density of “particles” at each point, and ask about how these particles are “moving” over
time; this idea can be made formal via the so-called continuity equation. Under this view, it
turns out that the inner expression ∂x u represents (up to a sign change) the flux (or momentum)
field, i.e. the total rate of particle movement at each point, and by taking the derivative of this
field, i.e. ∂x ∂x u, we determine whether, on the balance, there are more “incoming” or “outgoing”
particles—quantitatively, the rate of change ∂t u.
    With this perspective, a natural candidate PDE—which we call the directed heat equation—is

                                            ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u ,                                       (3)

where ∂x− u := min{0, ∂x u}. The idea is that the new flux ∂x− u is always nonpositive, which, up
to a required sign change, means that particles are only allowed to “move to the right”. Observe

                                                   5
also that the RHS of (3) is zero for any monotone function, so monotone functions are stationary
solutions, while for decreasing functions, this PDE behaves exactly as the heat equation. Thus,
intuitively, this PDE seems to take a function u(0) and move it toward a monotone limit over time.
    We explained above that the Poincaré inequality is intimately connected to convergence of the
heat equation, and we would like to show a similar property in the directed case. One option would
be to study the rate of decay of the distance to monotonicity, distmono
                                                                   2    . Although this is possible, it
does not seem to lead to a proof strategy; also note that, while in the case of the variance we also
have tools such as Fourier analysis to reason about the rate of decay directly, here we only have
the PDE to work with. Fortunately, there is another relevant quantity which decays exponentially
under the heat equation, namely the Dirichlet energy
                                                 Z
                                               1
                                       E(f ) =     (∂x f )2 dx .
                                               2
Indeed, another formal computation shows that by differentiating under the integral, switching the
partial derivatives, applying (2) and integrating by parts, we get
                  Z                 Z                         Z
              1
 ∂t E(u(t)) = ∂t (∂x u(t))2 dx = (∂x u(t))(∂t ∂x u(t)) dx = (∂x u(t))(∂x ∂t u(t)) dx
              2
             Z                               Z                       Z
                                                          2        1                    2
            = (∂x u(t))(∂x ∂x ∂x u(t)) dx = − (∂x ∂x u(t)) dx ≤ −      (∂x u(t))2 dx = − E(u(t)) ,
                                                                   C                    C
where the inequality is an application of a version of the Poincaré inequality for functions that are
zero on the boundary, which will be the case for ∂x u(t) by the no-flux boundary conditions.
    Now, it seems reasonable to propose the following directed analogue of the Dirichlet energy:
                                                  Z
                                                1
                                     E − (f ) =     (∂x− f )2 dx .
                                                2

If E measures the “locally non-constant” activity of a function, E − measures the “locally non-
monotone” activity. Using E − to recast the directed heat equation in the language of gradient
flows and maximal monotone operators [Bre73], we can show that

   1. this PDE has a solution (Proposition 5.17 and Corollary 5.20);

   2. the directed Dirichlet energy decays exponentially in time (Proposition 5.22);

   3. the solution converges to a monotone equilibrium as t → ∞ (Proposition 5.65); and

   4. the solution, including up to the limit above, satisfies several other essential analytic proper-
      ties, such as nonexpansiveness (Proposition 5.69) and order preservation (Corollary 5.68).

1.2.3    Transport-energy inequality in one dimension
It is tempting, now, to try and use these results to conclude the one-dimensional directed Poincaré
inequality. The problem is that this seems to lead to a dead end, because we do not know how to
tensorize the one-dimensional inequality into a multidimensional one5.
     Instead, we keep leaning on what the PDEs naturally tell us. It turns out that there is a
different notion of distance, the Wasserstein distance, which is much more closely connected to
   5
    In the classical case, the law of total variance does allow such tensorization [BGL14, Chapter 4.3], but this aspect
of the problem does not seem to be robust to passing to the directed setting. Also note that, if we only desired a
one-dimensional directed Poincaré inequality, then [Fer23] offers a much shorter proof.


                                                           6
the theory of evolution equations and better suited to the dynamical approach we are undertaking.
Informally, the squared Wasserstein distance W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) between probability measures ϱ0 and ϱ1 is
the minimum total cost of a “transport plan” (coupling) moving particles from ϱ0 to ϱ1 , where the
cost of moving a particle from point x to point y is |x − y|2 . The connection to PDEs is via the
Benamou-Brenier formula, which informally says that
                        Z 1                                                                            
   W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) = min      ∥vt ∥2L2 (ϱt ) dt : vt is a velocity field taking ϱ0 to ϱ1 from time 0 to 1 .
                         0

Without going into details, it follows that if we can upper bound the directed Dirichlet energy
E − (u(t)), then we can upper bound the total (weighted) magnitude of the velocity field, i. e.
∥vt ∥2L2 (ϱt ) (recall that the momentum of particles in the directed heat equation is essentially ∂x− u,
so this connection is not arbitrary), and therefore upper bound the Wasserstein distance between
the initial state ϱ0 —which informally corresponds to the function f = u(0)—and the final state
ϱ1 —which informally corresponds to the monotone equilibrium f ∗ = limt→∞ u(t).
     This strategy yields, at least for bounded functions, the following result. Let U be a class of
“reasonable” initial states which we do not define for now, and let the operator P∞ map each initial
state to its monotone equilibrium according to the directed heat equation. Then

Theorem 6.11 (Transport-energy inequality in one dimension). There exists a constantR C > 0 such
that the following holds. Let u ∈ U be positive, bounded away from zero, and satisfy (0,1) u dx = 1.
Define the measures dµ := u dx and dµ∞ := (P∞ u) dx. Then

                                                          C −
                                      W22 (µ, µ∞ ) ≤          E (u) .
                                                        inf u

1.2.4   Tensorizing the transport-energy inequality
The advantage of making this detour through the Wasserstein distance is that, modulo the required
technical work, tensorization becomes relatively straightforward. Indeed, it is well-known in the
theory of optimal transport (c. f. [Vil09, Remark 6.6]) that the W2 distance scales well with the
dimension and reflects useful geometric content. Here the main idea is that, since the cost function
for the W2 distance is the squared Euclidean distance |x − y|2 between points x, y ∈ [0, 1]d , one way
to extend the one-dimensional result above to, say, the unit square [0, 1]2 is to

   1. apply the one-dimensional result to each row, making the row restrictions monotone while
      paying cost W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) = a2 ; and then

   2. apply the one-dimensional result to each column, making the column restrictions monotone
      while paying cost W22 (ϱ1 , ϱ2 ) = b2 .

Then, by combining the transport of particles along rows and columns into a single transport plan,
we obtain via the Pythagorean theorem a plan with cost a2 + b2 , so this quantity upper bounds the
overall squared distance W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ2 ). The same idea extends to higher dimensions, and we prove:

Lemma 6.34 (Pythagorean composition of transport plans). Let I, J ⊆ [d] be nonempty disjoint
sets. Let µ, ϱ, ν ∈ P (Rd ) be supported in bounded sets, and let γ + ∈ ΠI (µ → ϱ) and γ − ∈ ΠJ (ϱ →
ν). Then there exists γ ∈ ΠI∪J (µ → ν) satisfying C2 (γ)2 = C2 (γ + )2 + C2 (γ − )2 .

   In this statement, C2 (γ)2 is the cost of transport plan γ, and ΠI (µ → ν) denotes the set of
transport plans between probability measures µ to ν, under two additional restrictions: 1) particles

                                                    7
can only move along “I-aligned” lines, e. g. along rows in the example; and 2) particles can only
move “up” in the partial order on Rd , i. e. a particle can move from x to y only if x ⪯ y—we call
such a transport plan directed. The second restriction is important because, when we finally recover
a Poincaré inequality from a transport-energy inequality, the information that particles only moved
up in the partial order will be reflected in the appearance of the directed gradient ∇− .
    Now, informally, Theorem 6.11 tells us that each step of the above strategy (e. g. moving
                                                                                        R − particles
along rows) incurs cost bounded by the directed Dirichlet energy, i. e. the integral (∂i f )2 dx of
the partial derivatives along the (say) rows. Repeating for every i ∈ [d] via Lemma 6.34, we obtain
the directed, multidimensional transport-energy inequality:

Theorem 6.51 (Transport-energy inequality). There exists a universal constant CR > 0 such that
the following holds. Let a ∈ (0, 1), and let f ∈ Lip satisfy 1 − a ≤ f ≤ 1 + a and [0,1]d f dx = 1.
Define the probability measures dµ := f dx and dµ∗ := f ∗ dx on [0, 1]d . Then

                                             C(1 + a)2
                                                        Z
                                       ∗                            2
                              2
                           W2 (µ → µ ) ≤              3
                                                                ∇− f dx .
                                              (1 − a)    [0,1]d

    Moreover, by the order preservation property of our solution to our PDE, each operation along
direction i preserves the monotonicity along the directions j < i, so the final function f ∗ in the
statement above, which is the result after all d steps, is indeed monotone. The notation W (µ → µ∗ )
indicates that this bound holds even for directed transport plans, as explained above.

1.2.5   Recovering a Poincaré inequality via optimal transport duality
The final step is to recover, from the multidimensional transport-energy inequality, our desired
directed Poincaré inequality. Fortunately, there is an intimate connection between transport and
Poincaré inequalities (as well as Sobolev, logarithmic Sobolev, and other isoperimetric and con-
centration inequalities), and this theory is well established in the classical case (c. f. [Vil09, Chap-
ters 21 and 22]). Closest to the present approach, [Liu20] proved, in the classical setting, the
equivalence between transport-energy, Poincaré, and other related inequalities. Our task, there-
fore, is to obtain at least an implication in the directed setting.
    The main ingredient toward this goal is the notion of Kantorovich duality for the Wasserstein
distance. In the classical setting, the weak Kantorovich duality says that, given two probability
measures µ and ν, if we can find “certificate functions” ϕ and ψ satisfying

                          ϕ(y) − ψ(x) ≤ |x − y|2         for all points x and y,                    (4)

then                                        Z                  Z
                             W22 (µ, ν) ≥       ϕ(y) dν(y) −       ψ(x) dµ(x) .                     (5)

Villani recounts the following analogy by Caffarelli: suppose you operate a consortium of bakeries
and cafes in a city, and that µ(dx) is the supply of bread by a bakery at point x while ν(dy)
is the demand by a cafe at point y. Then if transporting a unit of bread from x to y costs
|x − y|2 , the minimum cost for transporting all the bread is by definition W22 (µ, ν). Suppose
a transportation company offers to take over the transportation job by buying bread from the
bakeries at price ψ(x) and selling it to cafes at price ϕ(y). If (4) holds then, for each x and y,
hiring the transportation company is no more expensive than handling the transportation yourself.
Therefore the cost W22 (µ, ν) is at least as large as how much the bakery-cafe consortium pays the
company to fulfill the supply and demand, which is the RHS of (5).

                                                     8
     In the directed setting, where we can only transport mass from x to y if x ⪯ y, we expect the
transport to be in general more expensive, so “more” certificates should be valid. Indeed, it is not
difficult to obtain the following natural directed version of weak duality (Lemma 7.1): if

                               ϕ(y) − ψ(x) ≤ |x − y|2         for all points x ⪯ y,

then                                             Z                  Z
                                W22 (µ → ν) ≥        ϕ(y) dν(y) −       ψ(x) dµ(x) .

   The final main ingredient is an operator which, given a candidate function ϕ, produces the best
possible ψ for duality. This is accomplished by a directed analogue of the so-called Hamilton-Jacobi
operator, defined as follows: for each function h on the unit cube,
                                     (
                         ⃗ t h)(x) := h(x) 

                                                       1       2
                                      supy⪰x h(y) − 2t |x − y|     otherwise.

Then, for each h and setting t = 1, this operator yields the following (Proposition 7.4):
                                               Z         Z
                              1
                                W 2
                                    (µ →  ν) ≥   h dν −     ⃗ 1 h) dµ .
                                                           (H                                                  (6)
                              2 2


                        ⃗ t h)(x) intuitively seeks the direction y − x ⪰ 0 of steepest ascent of h.
Note that, as t → 0+ , (H
This suggests a connection to the directed gradient, and indeed in Proposition 7.6 we show that

                                               ⃗ t h(x) − h(x)

where ∇+ h := max{0, ∇h}.
    At this point, instead of trying to reproduce the calculations from Section 7.2, let us give some
intuition for how the pieces above recover a Poincaré inequality. By homogeneity, it suffices to
consider mean-zero functions h. The key idea is to fix a small t > 0 and let h play two roles at the
same time: 1) as the building block for measures dµ(x) = (1 + th) dx and dµ∗ (x) = (1 + th∗ ) dx,
where h∗ is the monotone function obtained via the coordinate-wise application of the directed heat
equation as in the previous section; and 2) in the test function −th for duality via (6).
R From    the fact that h plays both of these roles6, (6)Rends up producing the “interaction term”
  (h2 −hh∗ )R dx, which can be appropriately bounded by (h−h∗ )2 dx; and since h∗ is monotone, the
inequality (h − h∗ ) dx ≥ distmono

to appear. Moreover, after the appropriate calculations, the term involving H  ⃗ in (6) gives rise to

                                                                    R

Theorem 6.51 gives that W22 (µ, µ∗ ) is upper bounded by an expression involving |∇− h|2 dx.
Therefore, chaining the inequalities, we have precisely the terms required to put (1) together.

1.3      Discussion and open questions
Conceptual and technical aspects. We consider the dynamical approach to Theorem 1.1—
which establishes that the convergence properties of a PDE underlies both directed and classical
isoperimetric statements—to be the main conceptual contribution of this work. The role played by
  6
      We may think of test function −th as the transportation company trying to profit from non-monotonicity of h.


                                                         9
optimal transport speaks to the intimate relation between optimal transport and such dynamical
processes, and the fact that some of the optimal transport theory seems to find natural directed
counterparts is also intriguing and could be of independent interest.
    Much of our technical effort is in dealing with the nonlinear nature of the ∇− operator. For
example, in principle this rules out well-known Fourier analytic arguments. In fact, even many of
the tools from nonlinear PDEs fail to apply at first, because they require some sort of coercivity
that is not satisfied in our setting—at a very informal level, because ∂x− u may remain at zero even as
∂x u grows arbitrarily large, which “opens the door” for pathological objects to obstruct the theory.
We deal with this difficulty via a canonical decomposition u = u↑ + u↓ of u into a nondecreasing
u↑ and a nonincreasing u↓, so that we can isolate the phenomena we can control in u↓ (and in fact
recover a bit of linearity), and deal with the less well-behaved u↑ only when necessary.
Comparison with [Fer23]. In prior work, [Fer23] gave an L1 tester for functions f satisfying
Lip1 (f ) ≤ L with query complexity O(dL/ε), where Lip1 (f ) is the Lipschitz constant of f with
respect to the ℓ1 metric. In contrast, in this paper we parameterize the problem by the more
natural ℓ2 (Euclidean)
               √           metric7. Our tester takes functions satisfying Lip2 (f ) ≤ M and has query
complexity O( dM /ε ), and it is also a fortiori an L1 tester as already remarked.
             e        2   2

    By monotonicity
           √            of ℓq norms and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, in general we have Lip1 (f ) ≤
Lip2 (f ) ≤ d Lip1 (f ), which allows rough comparisons between the two results. Since the Euclidean
geometry is more natural than the ℓ1 geometry, we find the first inequality more informative: it
implies that the tester of [Fer23] is also an L1 tester for functions satisfying Lip2 (f ) ≤ M with
query complexity O(dM/ε). Thus, for L1 testing M -Lipschitz functions and ignoring logarithmic
                                                                    ε
factors, Theorem 1.2 is better than the tester of [Fer23] when M      > √1d and vice-versa. Curiously,
something analogous is true of the testers of [KMS18] and [GGLRS00] for the Boolean setting.
    [Fer23] asked about lower bounds (for general testers) in the present setting, and this question
remains open. We also do not resolve Conjecture 1.8 of [Fer23], which asks for a directed (L1 , ℓ2 )-
Poincaré inequality for Lipschitz f : [0, 1]d → R, √i. e. an analogue to the inequalities of Talagrand
and Bobkov & Houdré, but rather obtain our O( dM 2 /ε2 ) tester via the (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality.
                                                  e

Comparison with the path tester. As explained in Section 1.1, our directional derivative
tester is essentially the natural continuous analogue of the path tester of [KMS18], where taking
edge i in the path tester roughly corresponds to letting vi = 1 in the directional derivative query
v · ∇f (x). This conceptual connection is intriguing, and we do not know whether there is a formal
connection between the continuous and discrete problems that could explain it.
Nonlinear discrete Laplacian. In the study of spectral theory for directed graphs, [Yos16]
introduced a notion of nonlinear Laplacian operator, with an associated heat equation, and applied
these ideas to network analysis and spectral clustering of directed graphs. Our directed heat
equation may be seen as a closely related continuous counterpart to the dynamical process studied
in [Yos16], and further exploring this connection is an exciting direction for future work.
Further applications? Finally, we ask whether other problems in property testing—particularly
in the case of continuous domain—can benefit from the techniques and ideas in the proof of The-
orem 1.1. In property testing, the idea of comparing the input object f to some “ideal” object
f ∗ that satisfies the property is very natural, and in this paper we offer techniques from partial
differential equations and optimal transport as useful tools for reasoning about this comparison
smoothly in time when the problem is continuous in nature. Therefore, it is plausible that these
tools may have something to say about other property testing problems of continuous nature.
  7
                        √
      In fact, that a       d tester would be parameterized in the ℓ2 metric if it exists was already suggested in [Fer23].


                                                                10
Organization. The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we introduce definitions
and conventions used throughout the paper. In Section 3, we give our monotonicity tester and prove
Theorem 1.2. In Section 4, we prove the query complexity lower bound for derivative-pair testers.
Finally, Sections 5 to 7 establish Theorem 1.1, following the outline given in the proof overview.


2    Preliminaries
In this paper, N denotes the set of strictly positive integers {1, 2, . . . }. Throughout the paper,
d ∈ N is an arbitrary natural number indicating the dimension of the ambient space Rd unless
otherwise specified. For m ∈ N, we write [m] to denote the set {i ∈ N : i ≤ m}. For any x ∈ R, we
write x+ for max{0, x} and x− for max{0, −x}, and we extend this notation to vectors u ∈ Rd in
                                                                   −
the natural way: u+ , u− ∈ Rd are given by u+              +                −
                                                 i := (ui ) and ui := (ui ) . For real numbers a and
b, we use the notation a ∧ b := min(a, b) and a ∨ b := max(a, b).
     For a vector u ∈ Rd , we let supp(u) ⊆ [d] denote the set of indices where u is nonzero, and write
∥u∥0 := |supp(u)|. For two points x, y ∈ Rd , we write x ⪯ y if xi ≤ yi for every i ∈ [d], and y ⪰ x
if x ⪯ y.
                                               d
     We denote the closure of a set  R D ⊂ R by D. For a measure space (Ω, Σ, µ) and measurable
function f : Ω → R, we write Ω f dµ for the Lebesgue integral of f over this space when it
exists. Then for 1 ≤ p < +∞, Rthe space Lp (Ω) is the set of measurable functions f such that
|f |p is Lebesgue integrable, i. e. Ω |f |p dµ < +∞, and we write the Lp norm of such functions as
                         1/p
∥f ∥Lp (Ω) = Ω |f |p dµ       . We usually write dx for the Lebesgue measure on Rd in the context of
               R

integration, i. e. for Lebesgue measurable set Ω ⊂ Rd , we write Ω f dx for the Lebesgue integral of
                                                                   R

integrable f . When we need to refer to the Lebesgue measure of a set Ω explicitly, we write L(Ω).
     Throughout the paper, all measures on Rd are Borel measures. We say that measure µ over
Ω ⊂ Rd is absolutely continuous if it is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure,
i.e. if there exists a measurable function f : Ω → R≥0 satisfying dµ = f dx. In this case, f is called
the Radon-Nikodym derivative, or density of µ.
     Given a set Ω ⊂ Rd and M > 0, we say f : Ω → R is M -Lipschitz if |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M |x − y|
for all x, y ∈ Ω. The Lipschitz constant of f is the smallest M for which f is M -Lipschitz. We say
that f is Lipschitz if it is M -Lipschitz for any M > 0.
                             ?      ?
   We use the notation a ≤ b, a = b, etc. within a proof to denote (in)equalities that have not yet
been established.

Notation for directed partial derivatives and gradients. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be an open set, and
let f : Ω → R be Lipschitz. Then by Rademacher’s theorem f is differentiable almost everywhere
in Ω. For each x ∈ Ω where f is differentiable, let ∇f (x) = (∂1 f (x), . . . , ∂d f (x)) denote its
gradient, where ∂i f (x) is the partial derivative of f along the i-th coordinate at x. Then, let
∂i− := 0 ∧ ∂i , i. e. for every x where f is differentiable we have ∂i− f (x) = − (∂i f (x))− . We call ∂i−
the directed partial derivative operator in direction i. Then we define the directed gradient operator
by ∇− := (∂1− , . . . , ∂d− ), again defined on every x where f is differentiable. Note that ∇− f ⪯ 0.
We also similarly define ∂i+ := 0 ∨ ∂i and ∇+           +            +
                                                 i := (∂1 , . . . , ∂d ).

4     Lower bound
Definition 4.1. A derivative-pair tester for monotonicity of Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R is
described by a distribution D over pair tests and directional derivative tests, where
    1. a pair test is an operation that performs a pair of value queries f (x), f (y) for x ⪯ y, and
       rejects if and only if f (x) > f (y); and

    2. a directional derivative test is an operation that performs a directional derivative query at
       point x and direction v ⪰ 0, and rejects if and only if ∇f · v < 0.
The tester independently samples m tests from D, rejects if any of these tests rejects, and accepts
otherwise. By definition, every derivative-pair tester is nonadaptive and has one-sided error.

Theorem 4.2. Every L1 derivative-pairtester
                                        √     for monotonicity of M -Lipschitz functions f :
      d                                   dM
[0, 1] → R must have query complexity Ω   ε   .

                                                        16
How to read the rest of this paper. Sections 5 to 7 establish the proof of Theorem 1.1 via
arguments from partial differential equations and optimal transport theory, and make up the most
technical portion of this paper. While we give technical preliminaries summarizing the standard
theory we require in the beginning of Sections 5 and 6, for a detailed reading it may also be helpful
to have the listed reference texts [Eva10; Bre11; Vil09; San15] on hand.
    We favour a linear presentation in which results are built up progressively (occasionally deferring
a single-use lemma to be given immediately after the result that requires it), culminating in one
or a few key results in each subsection – these are indicated at the start of each section. While we
defer a few technical lemmas to Appendix A, we include in the main text the proofs of lemmas that
refer to the particular theory developed in this paper (as opposed to standard results of general
interest). Therefore, it may be convenient to skip proofs of lemmas on a first reading.


5    Directed heat semigroup
In this section, we make concrete the ideas presented in Section 1.2.2 of the proof overview. Recall
that we wish to analyze the directed heat equation, which we informally specified in (3) as

                                            ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u .                                      (8)

We require a more rigorous definition before we can study this PDE, since e. g. the quantity ∂x− u =
min{0, ∂x u} is not in general differentiable even if u is smooth. Indeed, while the classical heat
equation has a smoothing effect, we expect any solution to (8) (in the appropriate sense) to have
non-differentiable points, so a more technical treatment of this nonlinear PDE is unavoidable.
    The first step is to replace the operator ∂ − u with something more amenable to analysis. In
Definition 5.8 we define a canonical representation u = u↑ + u↓ such that u↓ is nonincreasing and
differentiable (more precisely, it is in the Sobolev space H 1 ), while u↑ is nondecreasing and need
not be differentiable (it only needs to be in the space L2 of square-integrable functions). While a
priori such a representation need not be unique, we show in Proposition 5.6   R that there is a unique
representation (after fixing the shift by a constant) minimizing the energy (∂x u↓)2 dx; intuitively,
this means that we pack all the “decreasing behaviour” of u into u↓, and all the “increasing
behaviour” of u into u↑. Note that u↑ may even contain jump discontinuities, which our theory
must withstand, so this decomposition captures extremely relevant properties of our problem.
    Then, we are able to restate our PDE more rigorously in Definitions 5.11 and 5.15. Essentially,
the “Neumann problem”                   (
                                          z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in (0, 1)
                                          ∂x u↓ = 0    on {0, 1}
given in (10) (where z will take the role of ∂t u in Definition 5.15) replaces ∂x− u with ∂x u↓ in (8),
and then places a “Neumann boundary condition” ∂x u↓ = 0 which, as is standard in PDEs, means
that there will be no mass flux at the boundaries of the domain. For technical reasons that are
common to most study of PDEs, we need to give in (11) a notion of “weak solution”, which is
essentially a solution which may not be regular enough to satisfy the specification of the PDE (i. e.
it may not be twice-differentiable), but which behaves (via integration by parts) just like a “strong
solution” for many analytical purposes, and can sometimes be “promoted”: a weak solution that
is regular enough is usually also a strong solution (in fact, our problem automatically enjoys such
a promotion thanks to “elliptic regularity”, see Lemma 5.21).
    To study our PDE, we bring in the theory of gradient flows and maximal monotone operators
[Bre73] by recasting the PDE as the problem of finding the direction of steepest descent (in the space


                                                   17
of L2 functions) of the directed Dirichlet energy E − (u), which our formalism allows us to define in
terms of u↓ in Definition 5.9. The main results in Section 5.2 are Proposition 5.17 on the existence
of a unique solution to the gradient flow problem – which allows us to define a semigroup operator
Pt , the directed heat semigroup, describing the evolution of this solution – and Corollary 5.20, which
shows that this solution is also a solution to the Neumann problem (i. e. our PDE of interest).
     To conclude the basic study of our PDE, Section 5.3 shows, via the theory of gradient flows,
that the directed Dirichlet energy E − (Pt u) decays exponentially in time (Proposition 5.22), which
allows us to conclude that Pt u converges to some limit as t → ∞ (Proposition 5.24).
     Sections 5.4 to 5.6 are concerned with showing that regular initial states u yield solutions Pt u
that are also regular for all times t > 0, where “regular” can mean Lipschitz or Sobolev class H 1 .
The main result in Section 5.4 is Lemma 5.25, a technical lemma which says that for solutions to
our PDE, if ∂x u↓ < 0 in an interval (a, b) (which we wish to call “u is decreasing”), it must be
the case that u↑ is constant in (a, b) (legitimizing that wish). This result is far from being vacuous
or tautological – it implies that in regions where our PDE is “active” (because ∂x u↓ is nonzero),
the other component u↑ of u is “inactive” (constant), which means that solutions to our PDE
avoid a host of pathological scenarios and allows crucial technical arguments to go through. Then,
using technical arguments and tools from the theory of maximal monotone operators, Section 5.5
builds toward Corollary 5.39 – H 1 regularity is preserved over time – and Section 5.6 builds toward
Corollary 5.45 – Lipschitz regularity is preserved over time.
     In Section 5.7, we turn our attention to nonexpansiveness and order preservation properties. The
main result, Proposition 5.54, is a quantitative order preservation result which implies that if initial
states u, v satisfy u ≤ v, then Pt u ≤ Pt v for all times t > 0 (Corollary 5.56). We conclude Section 5.7
with some useful basic observations about the semigroup Pt ; in particular, Proposition 5.63 shows
that Pt behaves nicely under certain affine transformations, namely Pt (αu + β) = αPt u + β when
α > 0 and β ∈ R.
     Finally, Section 5.8 studies the convergence of solutions Pt u as t → ∞. The main result is
Proposition 5.65, which shows that Pt u converges to a monotone function, which we define as
P∞ u in Definition 5.66. The remainder of Section 5.8 shows that the main properties of Pt shown
in the previous subsections – preservation of regularity, nonexpansiveness, behaviour under affine
transformations – are also satisfied by P∞ .

5.1   Preliminaries for PDE
We briefly outline some of the main concepts required to study our PDE, and refer the reader to
e.g. [Eva10; Bre11] for detailed expositions.
     Let J = (a, b) be an open interval. The absolutely continuous (AC) functions f : J → R
are precisely
        R       those for which there exists a function g ∈ L1 (J) such that, for all x ∈ J, f (x) =
f (a) + (a,x) g dx. We call g a weak derivative of f , and write ∂x f for any weak derivative of f . The
weak derivative is almost everywhere (a.e.) uniquely determined, and moreover, f is classically
differentiable a. e. and its classical derivative agrees with ∂x f a. e. .
     Let Ω := J d . Let k ∈ Z≥0 and p ∈ [1, +∞]. The Sobolev space W k,p (Ω) is the space of functions
f : Ω → R which have weak derivatives up to order k in Lp (Ω). The definition of weak derivative
in the multidimensional case is more involved than in the one-dimensional case, but the details are
not relevant here. In one dimension, we may recursively define W 0,p (J) := Lp (J) and, for k ≥ 1,
W k,p (J) as the set of functions f ∈ W k−1,p (J) such that ∂x f ∈ W k−1,p (J).
     We identify a. e. equal functions into equivalence classes in W k,p (Ω), and we write “f = g a.e.”
and “f = g in W k,p (Ω)” interchangeably. Often, we are interested in a continuous representative of
f , which is unique when it exists, and by abuse of notation write f for its continuous representative


                                                   18
as well. With this interpretation, a standard fact is that W 1,∞ (Ω) is precisely the class of Lipschitz
functions on Ω. If 1 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ +∞, then W k,q (Ω) ⊂ W k,p (Ω).
    The case p = 2 is special because then H k (Ω) := W k,2 (Ω) is a Hilbert space. In one dimension,
the inner product in H k (J) is
                                                    k
                                                    X
                                 ⟨f, g⟩H k (J) :=          Di f, Di g L2 (J) ,
                                                    i=0

where Di above denotes the i-th weak derivative. When the space of integration is clear, we drop
the subscript from the inner product notation. The inner product above also induces the norm
                                               v
                                               u k
                                               uX
                                 ∥f ∥H k (J) = t   ∥Di f ∥2L2 (J) .
                                                      i=0


    For a sequence (fn )n∈N ⊂ H k (Ω) and f ∈ H k (Ω), the notation “f → g in H k (Ω)” means
convergence in norm, i. e. ∥fn − f ∥H k (Ω) → 0 as n → ∞. We write “f ⇀ g weakly in H k (Ω)” to
denote weak convergence in this space, which means that φ(fn ) → φ(f ) as n → ∞ for every φ in
the dual space of H k (Ω). The dual space of L2 (Ω) is L2 (Ω) itself with the L2 (Ω) inner product
action φ(f ) = ⟨φ, f ⟩. The dual space of H 1 (Ω) is larger, but contains L2 (Ω) with the same action.
    It is often useful to approximate a function by a sequence of smooth functions, and for that
we will use mollification. The following comes from [Eva10, Appendix C]. The standard mollifier
η ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) is given by                               

    Let U ⊂ Rd be an open set and let Uε := {x ∈ U : dist(x, ∂U ) > ε}, where ∂U is the boundary

we define the mollification f ε : Uε → R of f by
                                         Z                        Z

                                         U                             B(0,ε)

We then have the following properties (see Theorem 7 of [Eva10, Appendix C]):

  1. f ε ∈ C ∞ (Uε ).

  2. f ε → f a. e. as ε → 0.

  3. If f ∈ C(U ), then f ε → f uniformly on compact subsets of U .

  4. If 1 ≤ p < ∞ and f ∈ Lploc (U ), then f ε → f in Lploc (U ),



                                                      19
where Lploc (U ) is the space of measurable functions whose every restriction to compact K ⊂ U is
in Lp (K), and convergence in Lploc (U ) means convergence in Lp (K) for every compact K ⊂ U .
    We also use the following abstract spaces. Let X be a real Banach space (for us, typically
L2 (J)), let 1 ≤ p ≤ +∞ and let T ∈ (0, +∞]. Then the Bochner space Lp (0, T ; X) is the space
of Bochner measurable functions (whose precise definition is not important here) u : [0, T ] → X
whose norm ∥u∥Lp (0,T ;X) is finite, where
                                                              1/p

As usual, C([0, T ]; X) denotes the set of continuous functions u : [0, T ] → X. When X possesses
the so-called Radon-Nikodym property, which in particular is the case for X = L2 (J), and for
T < +∞, the characterization of absolutely continuous functions as those possessing an integrable
weak derivative introduced earlier extends to functions u : [0, T ] → X (see [DU77, pp. 217–219]).
    Above and hereafter, we use boldface symbols, e. g. u, to denote Banach-valued functions whose
domain we think of as the time in an evolution equation.
    Hereafter, we write I for the unit interval (0, 1) unless otherwise specified, which will always be
clearly indicated.

5.2   Neumann and gradient flow problems
Definition 5.1 (Down-H 1 functions). We define the set U of down-H 1 functions as

                                   U := {u ∈ L2 (I) : R(u) ̸= ∅} ,

where R(u) is the set of admissible representations for u as follows:
        n

Remark 5.2. The properties “nondecreasing” and “nonincreasing” in the definition above techni-
cally do not apply directly to members of L2 (I) and H 1 (I), which are equivalence classes of a. e.
equal functions. Rather, we mean is that u(1) is a member of L2 (I) which has a representative
function I → R that is nondecreasing, and likewise for u(2) . More generally, when f is an object
of any space that identifies a. e. equal functions, we abuse language and write “f is nonincreasing”
(resp. nondecreasing) to mean that f has a nonincreasing (resp. nondecreasing) representative.

Remark 5.3. Is is easy to check that H 1 (I) ⊂ U, since for any u ∈ H 1 (I) we may separate
the positive and negative parts of ∂x u into u(1) and u(2) , respectively, and shift u(1) and u(2) by
appropriate constants so that they satisfy the conditions of Definition 5.1. Then, since H 1 (I) is
dense in L2 (I), so is U.

Proof of uniqueness. We show uniqueness using strict convexity. Suppose (u(1) , u(2) ), (v (1) , v (2) ) ∈

definition of R(u). Thus ∂x u(2) ̸= ∂x v (2) in L2 (I).

Lemma 5.7 (See e. g. [Bre11, Corollary 3.23]). Let E be a reflexive Banach space and let C ⊆ E be
nonempty, closed and convex. Suppose f : C → (−∞, +∞] is convex, proper, lower semicontinuous,
and coercive in the sense that for every sequence (xn )n∈N in C with ∥xn ∥E → ∞, we have f (xn ) →
+∞. Then there exists x∗ ∈ C satisfying f (x∗ ) = inf x∈C f (x).

   Owing to Proposition 5.6, we may define for each u ∈ U a canonical representation and directed
Dirichlet energy (by a slight abuse of notation):

Definition 5.8 (Canonical representation). For each u ∈ U, the canonical representation of u is
the unique element of R∗ (u), denoted (u↑, u↓). We define the directed Dirichlet energy of u by
D− (u) := D− (u↑, u↓).

    The following definition, which extends the directed Dirichlet energy functional to all of L2 (I)
by assigning +∞ to functions outside of U, will enable us to find and study a solution to our PDE
using tools from the theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows. Our main reference
for this theory is [Bre73].

Definition 5.9 (Energy functional). Define the functional E − : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] by
                                         (
                                 −         D− (u) if u ∈ U
                               E (u) :=
                                           +∞       otherwise.

Proposition 5.10. The functional E − : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] is convex, proper and lower semicontin-
uous.

Proof. Properness is trivial, since e. g. 0 ∈ U and E − (0) = 0. Convexity also follows easily from the
convexity of U and D− .

to u in L2 (I). We must show that

Definition 5.11 (Static Neumann problem). Let u ∈ U and z ∈ L2 (I). We say that u, z form a
weak solution to the static Neumann problem
                                   (
                                     z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in I
                                                                                        (10)
                                     ∂x u↓ = 0    on {0, 1}
if for all ϕ ∈ H 1 (I) we have     Z                 Z
                                         zϕ dx = −       (∂x u↓)(∂x ϕ) dx .                          (11)
                                     I               I

Remark 5.12. The equation z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in (10) is also called the Poisson equation, while ∂x u↓ = 0
on {0, 1} is called the (homogeneous) Neumann boundary condition.

Definition 5.13 (Nice evolution function). Let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) and u′ : (0, +∞)           2
                                                                                        R → ′L (I).
                                                                     ′
We say that u is a nice evolution function (with weak derivative u ) if 1) u(t) = u(0)+ (0,t) u (s) ds
for all t > 0; 2) u′(δ,+∞) ∈ L∞ (δ, +∞; L2 (I)) for all δ > 0, where u′J denotes the restriction of u′
to domain J ⊆ (0, +∞); and 3) u′(0,δ) ∈ L2 (0, δ; L2 (I)) for all δ > 0.

Remark 5.14. Definition 5.13 captures functions that are Lipschitz away from zero, and absolutely
continuous with square-integrable weak derivative near zero.

Definition 5.15 (Neumann evolution problem). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be a
nice evolution function. We say u is a weak solution to the Neumann evolution problem with initial
state (or initial data) u0 if 1) u(0) = u0 ; 2) u(t) ∈ U for all t > 0; and 3) u(t), u′ (t) form a weak
solution to the static Neumann problem for a. e. t > 0.

    Before introducing the gradient flow problem, we need some notation. Let φ : L2 (I) → [0, +∞]
be a convex, proper, lower semicontinuous function. We write D(φ) := {u ∈ L2 (I) : φ(u) < +∞}
for the domain of φ. For any u ∈ L2 (I), we write define the subdifferential of φ at u as
                    ∂φ(u) := {z ∈ L2 (I) : ∀v ∈ L2 (I) . φ(v) ≥ φ(u) + ⟨z, v − u⟩} ,
and we write D(∂φ) := {u ∈ L2 (I) : ∂φ(u) ̸= ∅} for the domain of ∂φ. Using the fact that φ
is proper, it is easy to check that D(∂φ) ⊆ D(φ). It is standard that ∂φ is a maximal monotone
operator ; we will not use (the definition of) this property explicitly, but rather rely on the theory
of such operators as presented in [Bre73].
    We can now define the gradient flow problem:

Definition 5.16 (Gradient flow problem). Let u0 ∈ U and let u be a nice evolution function. We
say u is a solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state (or initial data) u0 if 1) u(0) = u0 ;
2) u(t) ∈ D(∂E − ) for all t > 0; and 3) u′ (t) ∈ −∂E − (u(t)) for a. e. t > 0.

   Note that in both Definitions 5.15 and 5.16, the pointwise condition at t = 0 makes sense by
the requirement that u be continuous.
   The theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows immediately yields that the
gradient flow problem has a unique solution, as follows:

Proposition 5.17. Let u0 ∈ U. Then there exists a unique solution u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) to the
gradient flow problem. Moreover, for all t > 0 we have
                                                 Z
                            −         −
                           E (u0 ) − E (u(t)) =        ∥u′ (s)∥2L2 (I) ds .
                                                              (0,t)


                                                         24
For u0 , v0 ∈ U, the corresponding solutions u, v have ∥u(t) − v(t)∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥u0 − v0 ∥L2 (I) for all
t > 0.

Proof. This is a direct application of [Bre73, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 and Proposition 3.1].

Directed heat semigroup. As described in [Bre73], the nonexpansive property of solutions u
allows us to define an operator Pt : L2 (I) → L2 (I) such that Pt u = u(t) for each initial state u ∈ U
with corresponding solution u, and with Pt u defined by continuous extension when u ∈ L2 (I) \ U
(recall that U is dense in L2 (I)). Then for every u ∈ L2 (I) and t > 0 it holds that Pt u ∈ D(∂E − ),
and (Pt )t≥0 forms a nonexpansive semigroup in L2 (I), i. e.

   1. Pt+s = Pt Ps for all t, s ∈ R≥0 , with P0 the identity;

   2. Pt u → u in L2 (I) as t ↓ 0, for all u ∈ L2 (I); and

   3. ∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥u − v∥L2 (I) for all u, v ∈ L2 (I) and t ∈ R≥0 .

The last property also implies that, for each t ≥ 0, Pt : L2 (I) → L2 (I) is continuous. We say that
Pt is the semigroup generated by −∂E − .
    It turns out that the solution to the gradient flow problem is also a weak solution to the
Neumann evolution problem, as the following results show.

Proposition 5.18. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ) and z ∈ −∂E − (u). Then u, z form a weak solution to the
static Neumann problem.

Corollary 5.20. Let u0 ∈ U and suppose u is a solution to the gradient flow problem. Then u is
also a weak solution to the Neumann evolution problem.

Proof. The condition u(0) = u0 holds by definition of solution to the gradient flow problem, and for
all t > 0 we have u(t) ∈ D(∂E − ) ⊆ D(E − ) = U. Finally, for a. e. t > 0 we have u′ (t) ∈ −∂E − (u(t)),
which by Proposition 5.18 implies that u(t), u′ (t) form a solution to the static Neumann problem.
Hence u is a weak solution to the Neumann evolution problem.

5.3   Exponential decay of directed Dirichlet energy
The following elliptic regularity result is standard, and essentially shows that weak solutions to
the static Neumann problem are in fact strong solutions whose regularity is two degrees higher
than that of z; in particular, even if we only have z ∈ L2 (I), we gain one degree of regularity by
obtaining u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) when we only assumed u↓ ∈ H 1 (I). Recall that H 0 (I) is the same as L2 (I).

Lemma 5.21 (Elliptic regularity; see [Mik78, Chapter IV Section 2, Theorems 3 and 4]). Let k ≥ 0
be an integer and let u ∈ U, z ∈ H k (I) form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem
                                      (
                                        z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in I
                                        ∂x u↓ = 0    on {0, 1} .

Then u↓ ∈ H k+2 (I), and moreover ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓ a. e. in I.

   We now give our energy decay result.

                                                   26
Proposition 5.22. There exists a constant K > 0 such that the following holds. Let u ∈ U. Then
for all t > 0,
                                   E − (Pt u) ≤ e−Kt E − (u) .

Proof. Let u ∈ U and let u(t) = Pt u be the corresponding solution to the gradient flow problem.
Recall that Proposition 5.17 gives, for all t > 0,
                                                   Z
                             −            −
                           E (u(t)) = E (u) +        −∥u′ (s)∥2L2 (I) ds .
                                                                (0,t)

It follows that t 7→ E − (u(t)) is absolutely continuous on every interval [0, T ] with weak derivative
∂t E − (u(t)) = −∥u′ (t)∥2L2 (I) . Moreover for a. e. t > 0 we have that u(t), u′ (t) form a weak solution
to the static Neumann problem, and thus u′ (t) = ∂x ∂x u(t)↓ a. e. in I and ∂x u(t)↓ = 0 on {0, 1} by
Lemma 5.21. Therefore the Poincaré inequality (for zero-on-the-boundary functions) yields

                            1                   1                       C
                E − (u(t)) = ∥∂x u(t)↓∥2L2 (I) ≤ C∥∂x ∂x u(t)↓∥2L2 (I) = ∥u′ (t)∥2L2 (I)
                            2                   2                       2
for some constant C > 0. It follows that for a. e. t > 0, we have
                                                                               2 −
                              ∂t E − (u(t)) = −∥u′ (t)∥2L2 (I) ≤ −               E (u(t)) ,
                                                                               C
which implies that for all t > 0,
                                            E − (u(t)) ≤ e−2t/C E − (u) .

    The exponential decay of t 7→ E − (Pt u) allows us to find a Cauchy sequence in (Pt u)t≥0 , and
thus establish its strong convergence to some limit in L2 (I). Later on, we will say more about this
limit by reasoning about the weak convergence of Pt u (to the same limit).

Lemma 5.23 (Cauchy sequence). Let u ∈ U, and let (uk )k∈N be given by uk := Pk u. Then (uk )k
is Cauchy as a sequence in L2 (I). As a consequence, uk → u∗ in L2 (I) for some u∗ ∈ L2 (I).

is a complete normed space. Let us now establish the Cauchy property. Fix any n > m in N. Then

Proposition 5.24 (Strong convergence). Let u ∈ U. Then Pt u converges in L2 (I) to some u∗ ∈
L2 (I) as t → ∞.

and hence ∥Pt u − u∗ ∥L2 (I) < 2ε. Thus Pt u → u∗ in L2 (I).

5.4      Auxiliary results for studying regularity of solutions
Lemma 5.25 (Functions in D(∂E − ) are well-behaved). Suppose u ∈ D(∂E − ). Let (a, b) ∈ I be a
nonempty interval and suppose that ∂x u↓(x) < 0 for all x ∈ (a, b). Then u↑ is constant in (a, b).

Proof. Let z ∈ L2 (I) be such that −z ∈ ∂E − (u), which is nonempty by hypothesis. By Proposi-
tion 5.18 and Lemma 5.21, we conclude that u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) (which in particular justifies writing the
condition that ∂x u↓ < 0 on (a, b), which we take to mean in terms of the continuous representative
of ∂x u↓ ∈ H 1 (I)).

                                                                   28
   By the continuity of ∂x u↓ and the boundary conditions ∂x u↓(0) = ∂x u↓(1) = 0 (which follows
from Lemma 5.21), we may assume without loss of generality (by extending the interval (a, b) if

Lemma 5.26. Let J ⊂ R be a finite, nonempty open interval. Let f ∈ L∞ (J) be nondecreasing
and let g ∈ H 1 (J) be such that g < 0 in J and g = 0 on ∂J, i. e. the endpoints of J. Let [a, b] ⊂ J,
let δ := f (b) − f (a) and let α := − sup(a,b) g. Then ⟨f, ∂x g⟩L2 (J) ≥ αδ.

Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that J = I. Now, for each sufficiently small
ε > 0, recall that Iε = (ε, 1 − ε) and let fε ∈ C ∞ (Iε ) be the mollification of f . Let fε∗ : I → R be
given by                                 
                                         fε/2 (ε)

                                         

                                         


                                                        30
Note that each fε∗ ∈ H 1 (I), in particular because the piecewise definition is continuous and fε/2 is
smooth on [ε, 1 − ε]. Also, each fε/2 is nondecreasing and hence so is each fε∗ . Moreover, fε∗ → f
a.e. as ε → 0 since this is true of (fε )ε>0 . Finally, we have fε∗ → f in L2loc (I) since fε → f in
L2loc (Ω), and since ∥fε∗ ∥L∞ (I) ≤ ∥fε/2 ∥L∞ (I) ≤ ∥f ∥L∞ (I) , we have that (fε∗ )ε>0 is bounded in L2 (I)
and Lemma A.2 implies that fε∗ ⇀ f weakly in L2 (I) as ε → 0.
     Let (an )n∈N , (bn )n∈N be two sequences such that an ↑ a, bn ↓ b, and moreover, for every n ∈ N,
fε (an ) → f (an ) and fε∗ (bn ) → f (bn ) as ε → 0; the existence of such sequences is guaranteed

by the fact that fε∗ → f almost everywhere. For each n ∈ N, let δn := f (bn ) − f (an ) and

that αn → α as n → ∞ by the continuity of g.

Lemma 5.27. The statement of Lemma 5.26 still holds if we replace the condition f ∈ L∞ (J) with
f ∈ L2 (J).

Proof. Again let J = I without loss of generality. We proceed by an approximation argument. Let
(an )n∈N be a strictly decreasing sequence satisfying a1 = a and an → 0. Similarly, let (bn )n∈N be a
strictly increasing sequence satisfying b1 = b and bn → 1. For each n ∈ N, define fn ∈ L∞ (I) by
                                         
                                         inf [an ,bn ] f if x ∈ (0, an )
                                         
                                fn (x) := f (x)           if x ∈ [an , bn ]
                                         
                                           sup[an ,bn ] f if x ∈ (bn , 1) .
                                         

Note that the infimum and supremum above are finite by virtue of the monotonicity of f and
the observation that each [an , bn ] ⊂ I; thus we indeed have fn ∈ L∞ (I). Moreover, each fn is
nondecreasing and, since an ≤ a < b ≤ bn , we have fn (a) = f (a) and fn (b) = f (b). Lemma 5.26
implies that ⟨fn , ∂x g⟩ ≥ αδ for α as in that statement and δ = fn (b) − fn (a) = f (b) − f (a). Finally,
we have fn → f in L2 (I); indeed, letting c := a+b 2 for convenience and using the monotonicity of f ,
