   The following auxiliary result confirms the natural expectation that, if u has a first derivative
and some degree of regularity, then its components u↑ and u↓ enjoy the same regularity and, more-
over, can be obtained by taking the positive and negative parts of the derivative ∂x u, respectively.

Proposition 5.28. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞, and let u ∈ U ∩W 1,p (I). Then u↑, u↓ ∈ W 1,p (I) and, moreover,
we have ∂x u↑ = ∂x+ u and ∂x u↓ = ∂x− u a. e. in I.

Proof. Let u(1) , u(2) : I → R be given by
                                Z                                                     Z
       u(1) (y) := u(0) + C +        ∂x+ u(x) dx             and   u(2) (y) := −C +            ∂x− u(x) dx
                                   (0,y)                                               (0,y)

for each y ∈ I, where C ∈ R is implicitly defined so as to satisfy
                                          Z
                                             u(2) dx = 0 .
                                                 I

Then u(1) is nondecreasing, u(2) is nonincreasing, both are absolutely continuous with ∂x u(1) = ∂x+ u
and ∂x u(2) = ∂x− u a. e. in I, and
                                             Z
                        (1)    (2)
                                                    ∂x+ u(x) + ∂x− u(x) dx = u(x)
                                                                       
                    (u + u )(y) = u(0) +
                                                     (0,y)

for all y ∈ I by the absolute continuity of u. It is also clear that u(1) , u(2) ∈ L∞ (I) ⊂ Lp (I), since
they are pointwise upper bounded in magnitude by |u(0)|+|C|+∥∂x u∥L1 (I) < +∞. Moreover, since
u ∈ W 1,p (I), we have ∂x u ∈ Lp (I) and hence ∂x+ u, ∂x− u ∈ Lp (I), yielding that u(1) , u(2) ∈ W 1,p (I).
    The proof will be concluded if we show that (u↑, u↓) = (u(1) , u(2) ). However, at this point we
cannot even state that (u(1) , u(2) ) ∈ R(u) because we have not established that u(2) ∈ H 1 (I) =
W 1,2 (I) (unless p ≥ 2, of course). However, if we can show that any (v (1) , v (2) ) ∈ R(u) satisfies
                                    Z                   Z
                                                      ?
                                      (∂x v (2) )2 dx ≥ (∂x u(2) )2 dx ,                                (13)
                                       I                      I

then using the assumption that u ∈ U and the definition of u↑, u↓, we will conclude that indeed
(u↑, u↓) = (u(1) , u(2) ), as needed.
    Let (v (1) , v (2) ) ∈ R(u). We claim that ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) for a. e. x ∈ I, which will imply
(13). Recall that v (2) ∈ H 1 (I) ⊂ W 1,1 (I). Since u = v (1) + v (2) a. e. and u ∈ W 1,p (I) ⊂ W 1,1 (I),
we conclude that v (1) ∈ W 1,1 (I). Hence u, u(1) , u(2) , v (1) , v (2) are all absolutely continuous. The
fundamental theorem of calculus for the Lebesgue integral implies that, almost everywhere in I,
these functions are all differentiable, and their classical and weak derivatives agree with ∂x u =
∂x u(1) + ∂x u(2) = ∂x v (1) + ∂x v (2) . On any such point x, the monotonicity of u(1) , u(2) , v (1) , v (2)
implies that ∂x u(1) (x), ∂x v (1) (x) ≥ 0 and ∂x u(2) (x), ∂x v (2) (x) ≤ 0. Therefore

        ∂x v (2) (x) = −∂x v (2) (x) = −∂x u(x) + ∂x v (1) (x) = −∂x u(2) (x) − ∂x u(1) (x) + ∂x v (1) (x)

                    = ∂x u(2) (x) − ∂x u(1) (x) + ∂x v (1) (x) .



                                                        32
Now, if ∂x u(x) ≥ 0, then ∂x u(2) (x) = 0 by definition, so ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) holds trivially.
Otherwise, we conversely have ∂x u(1) (x) = 0 while ∂x v (1) ≥ 0, and hence, by the above,

                                        ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) ,

which concludes the proof.

   The following standard facts will also be useful.

Fact 5.29 (See e. g. [EG15, Theorem 4.4]). Let 1 ≤ p < ∞ and let f ∈ W 1,p (I). Then ∂x f = 0
a. e. on {f = 0}.

   The following fact is an immediate application of the Sobolev embedding theorem:

Fact 5.30. Let f ∈ H 2 (I). Then f ∈ C 1,1/2 (I). In particular, f is continuously differentiable.

Fact 5.31 (See e. g. [Bar76, Chapter II, Corollary 2.1]). The set D(∂E − ) is a dense subset of
D(E − ) = U.

Observation 5.32. Since U contains H 1 (I), which is dense in L2 (I), Fact 5.31 implies that

                                    D(∂E − ) = D(E − ) = U = L2 (I) .

5.5    Preservation of H 1 regularity
We wish to show that if the initial state u is in H 1 (I), then Pt u remains in H 1 (I) for all times
t > 0. To that end, define φ : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] by
                                         (
                                           ∥∂x u∥2L2 (I) if u ∈ H 1 (I)
                                φ(u) :=
                                           +∞            otherwise.

   The theory of maximal monotone operators gives us a recipe to establish that t 7→ φ(Pt u) is
nonincreasing. The key ingredients are Claim 5.33 and Lemma 5.34.

Claim 5.33. The functional φ is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof. Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicontinuity.
Since L2 (I) is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity. Let (un )n∈N be
a sequence in L2 (I) such that un → u in L2 (I). We need to show that
                                                ?
                                         φ(u) ≤ lim inf φ(un ) .
                                                    n→∞

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subsequence
(unk )k∈N such that limk→∞ φ(unk ) = A < +∞. By extracting a subsequence if necessary, we may
assume that φ(unk ) < +∞, and thus unk ∈ H 1 (I), for every k.
    We claim that (unk )k is bounded in H 1 (I). Indeed suppose this is not the case. Then since
∥unk ∥2H 1 (I) = ∥unk ∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x unk ∥2L2 (I) and ∥∂x unk ∥2L2 (I) remains bounded due to the fact that
φ(unk ) → A, we conclude that ∥unk ∥L2 (I) gets arbitrarily large as k → ∞. But this contradicts the
fact that unk → u in L2 (I), so the claim holds.


                                                    33
   It follows that we may extract a weakly convergent subsequence (unkℓ )ℓ∈N , and by uniqueness of
weak limits, we obtain that u ∈ H 1 (I) and unkℓ ⇀ u weakly in H 1 (I). By weak lower semicontinuity
the norm in H 1 (I),

   ∥u∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x u∥2L2 (I) = ∥u∥2H 1 (I) ≤ lim inf ∥unkℓ ∥2H 1 (I) = lim inf ∥unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) .
                                                  ℓ→∞                       ℓ→∞

Since unkℓ → u in L2 (I), we have ∥unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) → ∥u∥2L2 (I) and hence

       φ(u) = ∥∂x u∥2L2 (I) ≤ −∥u∥2L2 (I) + lim inf ∥unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) = lim inf φ(unkℓ ) .
                                                    ℓ→∞                                       ℓ→∞

Lemma 5.34. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ) and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Then for all λ > 0, φ(u − λz) ≥ φ(u).

Proof. By Proposition 5.18, z is a weak solution to the static Neumann problem, and by Lemma 5.21
we have u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) with ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in L2 (I).
   Note that the result holds trivially if u − λz ̸∈ H 1 (I), in which case φ(u − λz) = +∞. Therefore
assume that u − λz ∈ H 1 (I). We consider four cases: u ∈ H 1 (I); u ∈ W 1,1 (I) \ H 1 (I), i. e. u is AC
but not in H 1 (I); u is continuous but not AC; and u is not continuous.8

Case 1. Suppose u ∈ H 1 (I). Note that in this case the assumption that u − λz ∈ H 1 (I) implies
that z ∈ H 1 (I) as well, so in particular ∂x u, ∂x z ∈ L2 (I). Additionally, Lemma 5.21 also yields
u↓ ∈ H 3 (I). We have
                        Z                                                 Z
           φ(u − λz) = (∂x (u − λz))2 dx = φ(u) + λ2 ∥∂x z∥2L2 (I) − 2λ (∂x u)(∂x z) dx .
                             I                                                          I

Therefore it suffices to show that            Z
                                                                    ?
                                                    (∂x u)(∂x z) dx ≤ 0 .                                          (14)
                                                I

Recalling that u = u↑ + u↓ with u↑, u↓ ∈ H 1 (I) = W 1,2 (I) by Proposition 5.28, we have
                    Z                  Z                     Z
                      (∂x u)(∂x z) dx = (∂x u↑)(∂x z) dx + (∂x u↓)(∂x z) dx ,
                         I                          I                       I

with the second term in the RHS satisfying
             Z                                Z
                                           1

                I                                         I

By Proposition 5.28, ∂x u↑ = ∂x+ u and ∂x u↓ = ∂x− u in L2 (I). Since z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in L2 (I), we get
                           Z                    Z
                             (∂x u↑)(∂x z) dx = (∂x+ u)(∂x ∂x ∂x u↓) dx .
                                  I                           I

We claim that the quantity above is zero. Indeed, fixing any representative of ∂x u ∈ L2 (I), let
S := {x ∈ I : ∂x u > 0}. First, we have (∂x+ u)(∂x ∂x ∂x− u) = ∂x+ u = 0 on I \ S. Second, we have
∂x u↓ = ∂x− u = 0 on S, so applying Fact 5.29 twice (recall that u↓ ∈ H 3 (I)) gives that ∂x ∂x ∂x u↓ = 0
a.e. in S, thus establishing the claim. Thus (14) indeed holds, which concludes the proof in Case 1.

We claim that (∂x u↑)(∂x z), (∂x u↓)(∂x z) ∈ L1 (I). First, we again have that (∂x u↑)(∂x z) =
(∂x u↑)(∂x ∂x ∂x u↓) = 0 a. e. as in the previous case, where in particular we are allowed to apply
Fact 5.29 twice to ∂x u↓ because ∂x u↓ ∈ W 2,1 (I) by virtue of the fact that ∂x ∂x u↓ = z ∈ W 1,1 (I) in
the current case. Hence (∂x u↑)(∂x z) ∈ L1 (I). Second, note that ∂x u↓ is AC and hence bounded,
while ∂x z ∈ L1 (I) since z is AC. Therefore
                         Z

               Z                    Z                       Z
                 |(∂x u)(∂x z)| dx ≤ |(∂x u↑)(∂x z)| dx + |(∂x u↓)(∂x z)| dx < +∞ ,
                  I                          I                     I

so (∂x u)(∂x z) ∈ L1 (I) as claimed. Now, since φ(u − λz) < +∞ by assumption, (17) shows that
(∂x u)2 −2λ(∂x u)(∂x z)+λ2 (∂x z)2 ∈ L1 (I), while we have just established that 2λ(∂x u)(∂x z) ∈ L1 (I).
We conclude that (∂x u)2 + λ2 (∂x z)2 ∈ L1 (I), i. e.
                                  Z

                      Z                                   Z

which is the desired contradiction. This concludes the proof in Case 2.

Case 4. Suppose u is not continuous. By Lemma 5.36, u − λz is not continuous, which implies
that u − λz ̸∈ H 1 (I) and φ(u − λz) = +∞, concluding the proof.

Lemma 5.35. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), suppose u is continuous, and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Let λ > 0 and let
v := u − λz. Let 0 < a < b < 1 and suppose u(a) < u(b). Then for all ε > 0, there exist a′ , b′ with
a ≤ a′ < b′ ≤ b such that
                                v(b′ ) − v(a′ ) ≥ |u(a) − u(b)| − ε .

Proof. Recall that, by Proposition 5.18, u, z form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem,
and by Lemma 5.21 we have u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) with ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in L2 (I). In
particular, u↓ is continuously differentiable by Fact 5.30.
   We first observe that it cannot be the case that ∂x u↓(x) < 0 for all x ∈ (a, b), since otherwise
Lemma 5.25 would imply that u↑ is constant in (a, b), which by continuity would imply that
u↑(a) = u↑(b) and hence u(a) ≥ u(b), a contradiction.
   We first construct a′ ∈ (a, b). In particular, we wish a′ to satisfy
                                          ?               ε                          ?
                                    u(a′ ) ≤ u(a) +                 and       z(a′ ) ≥ 0 .             (18)
                                                          2
Let x∗ ∈ [a, b) be given by
                                      x∗ := inf{x ∈ (a, b) : ∂x u↓(x) = 0} ,

                                                               36
which is well-defined by the observation above and the fact that ∂x u↓ ≤ 0 in all of I (since
u↓ is nonincreasing and continuously differentiable). By the continuity of ∂x u↓, we have that
∂x u↓(x∗ ) = 0. Since ∂x u↓(a) ≤ 0, it must be the case that either a = x∗ or

                                  L {x ∈ (a, x∗ ) : ∂x ∂x u↓(x) ≥ 0} > 0 .                             (19)

We consider each case separately. First, suppose a = x∗ . We claim that for all δ > 0, there exists
x ∈ (a, a + δ) such that z(x) ≥ 0. Suppose for a contradiction that this is not the case, and fix
δ > 0 such that for all x ∈ (a, a + δ), z(x) < 0. Then since z = ∂x ∂x u↓ almost everywhere, we
conclude that ∂x ∂x u↓ < 0 a. e. in (a, a + δ). Hence ∂x u↓(x) < 0 for all x ∈ (a, a + δ), contradicting
the assumption that a = x∗ given the definition of x∗ . Thus the claim holds. Now, using the
continuity of u, choose δ > 0 small enough and choose a′ ∈ (a, a + δ) so that z(a′ ) ≥ 0 and
moreover u(a′ ) ≤ u(a) + ε/2. This choice of a′ satisfies (18).
   Second, suppose a < x∗ and (19) holds. Since z = ∂x ∂x u↓ almost everywhere, choose a′ ∈ (a, x∗ )
such that z(a′ ) ≥ 0, which is possible by (19). Now, since ∂x u↓ < 0 for all x ∈ (a, a′ ) ⊂ (a, x∗ ) by
the choice of x∗ , Lemma 5.25 implies that u↑ is constant in (a, a′ ). By the continuity of u↑, we
conclude that u↑(a) = u↑(a′ ) and hence, since u↓ is nonincreasing, we have

                           u(a′ ) = u↑(a′ ) + u↓(a′ ) ≤ u↑(a) + u↓(a) = u(a) ,

and again (18) is satisfied. This concludes the choice of a′ .
    Now, we may assume without loss of generality that ε < |u(a) − u(b)|/2. Therefore our choice
of a′ yields an interval (a′ , b) which, using (18) and recalling that u(a) < u(b), satisfies u(a′ ) < u(b).
Therefore, repeating a symmetric version of the argument above yields a choice of b′ ∈ (a′ , b)
satisfying
                                                   ε
                                   u(b′ ) ≥ u(b) −      and      z(b′ ) ≤ 0 .                          (20)
                                                   2
Combining (18) and (20), we conclude that
                        h                i    h             i
       v(b′ ) − v(a′ ) = u(b′ ) − u(a′ ) − λ z(b′ ) − z(a′ ) ≥ u(b) − u(a) − ε = |u(a) − u(b)| − ε .

Lemma 5.36. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), suppose u is not continuous, and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Let λ > 0.
Then u − λz is not continuous.

Proof. Recall that, by Proposition 5.18, u, z form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem,
and by Lemma 5.21 we have u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) with ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in L2 (I). In
particular, u↓ is continuously differentiable by Fact 5.30.
    Since u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) is continuous while u is not continuous, we conclude that u↑ is not continuous
and, since it is monotone, it contains only jump discontinuities. Let x0 ∈ I be a point of jump
discontinuity of u↑, i. e. a point such that L− < L+ where

                          L− := lim u↑(x)          and      L+ := lim u↑(x) .
                                  x→x−
                                     0                              x→x+
                                                                       0


We first claim that ∂x u↓(x0 ) = 0. Indeed, it is clear that ∂x u↓ ≤ 0 since u↓ is nonincreasing and
continuously differentiable. If we had ∂x u↓(x0 ) < 0, then by continuity ∂x u↓ would be strictly
negative in a neighbourhood of x0 , in which case Lemma 5.25 would imply that u↑ is constant in
a neighbourhood of x0 , contradicting the fact that L− < L+ . Hence the claim holds.


                                                    37
    We now claim that, for all δ > 0, there exists x ∈ (x0 , x0 + δ) such that z(x) ≤ 0. Suppose for
a contradiction that there exists δ > 0 such that for all x ∈ (x0 , x0 + δ), z(x) > 0. Then, since
z = ∂x ∂x u↓ a. e. and ∂x u↓(x0 ) = 0, we conclude that ∂x u↓ > 0 in (x0 , x0 + δ), contradicting the
fact that ∂x u↓ ≤ 0. Hence the claim holds. By the same reasoning, we conclude that for all δ > 0
there exists x ∈ (x0 − δ, x0 ) such that z(x) ≥ 0.
    Thus, for all δ > 0 there exist points x− ∈ (x0 −δ, x0 ), x+ ∈ (x0 , x0 +δ) such that z(x+ )−z(x− ) ≤
0 and hence
                                     h                  i h                      i     h             i
   (u − λz)(x+ ) − (u − λz)(x− ) = u↑(x+ ) − u↑(x− ) + u↓(x+ ) − u↓(x− ) − λ z(x+ ) − z(x− )
                                     h                  i h                      i
                                   ≥ u↑(x+ ) − u↑(x− ) + u↓(x+ ) − u↓(x− ) .

Since u↑ → L+ as x ↓ x0 and u↑ → L− as x ↑ x0 and u↑ is nondecreasing, we have u↑(x+ )−u↑(x− ) ≥
L+ − L− . By the continuity of u↓, we can let δ > 0 be small enough so that u↓(x+ ) − u↓(x− ) ≥
− L+ −L
      2
         −
           . We conclude that, for all sufficiently small δ > 0, there exist points x− ∈ (x0 −δ, x0 ), x+ ∈
(x0 , x0 + δ) such that
                                                                  L+ − L−
                              (u − λz)(x+ ) − (u − λz)(x− ) ≥                ,
                                                                      2
and thus u − λz is not continuous.

Lemma 5.37 (Specialization of [Bre73, Theorem 4.4]). Let H be a Hilbert space and let τ : H →
[0, +∞] be a convex, proper, and lower semicontinuous functional such that τ (ProjD(A) x) ≤ τ (x)
for all x ∈ H. Let A : H → 2H be a maximal monotone operator and let St be the semigroup
generated by −A. Then the following are equivalent:
  1. τ ((I + λA)−1 x) ≤ τ (x) for all x ∈ H and λ > 0; and
  2. τ (St x) ≤ τ (x) for all x ∈ D(A) and t ≥ 0.

    In the statement above, (I + λA)−1 : H → D(A) is the resolvent of A (also denoted by Jλ , see
e.g. [Eva10, p. 563]).

Proposition 5.38 (φ-monotonicity of solutions). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the
solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then for all 0 ≤ t1 ≤ t2 < +∞, we have
φ(u(t1 )) ≥ φ(u(t2 )).

Proof. This is a direct consequence of Lemma 5.37. Indeed, letting H := L2 (I) and A := ∂E −
(which is maximal monotone as remarked earlier), we first observe that indeed φ(ProjD(A) f ) ≤ φ(f )
for all f ∈ L2 (I), since by Observation 5.32 we have ProjD(A) f = f . Thus it suffices to show that
for all f ∈ L2 (I) and all λ > 0,
                                                          ?
                                        φ((I + λA)−1 f ) ≤ φ(f ) .
But this is equivalent to showing that, for all u ∈ D(A), −z ∈ A(u) and λ > 0,
                                                 ?
                                           φ(u) ≤ φ(u − λz) ,
which is precisely Lemma 5.34.

Corollary 5.39 (Preservation of H 1 regularity). Suppose u0 ∈ H 1 (I), and let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I))
be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then u(t) ∈ H 1 (I) for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 5.38 and the definition of φ.

                                                     38
5.6   Preservation of Lipschitz regularity
It will also be useful to control the Lipschitz regularity of solutions. At a high level, we follow
a similar strategy as in Section 5.5. Recall that W 1,∞ (I) is equivalent to the space of Lipschitz
real-valued functions on I, up to identification of almost everywhere equal functions. Define ψ :
L2 (I) → [0, +∞] by                    (
                                        ∥∂x u∥L∞ (I) if u ∈ W 1,∞ (I)
                              ψ(u) :=
                                        +∞            otherwise.

Fact 5.40. Let u ∈ L2 (I) and let M ∈ R≥0 . Then ψ(u) ≤ M if and only if u = f a. e. for some
M -Lipschitz function f : I → R. Moreover, in this case f is the (unique) continuous representative
of u and ψ(u) is the Lipschitz constant of f .

Claim 5.41. The functional ψ is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof. Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicontinuity.
Since L2 (I) is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity. Let (un )n∈N be
a sequence in L2 (I) such that un → u in L2 (I). We need to show that
                                                ?
                                        ψ(u) ≤ lim inf ψ(un ) .
                                                    n→∞

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subsequence
(unk )k∈N such that limk→∞ ψ(unk ) = M < +∞. By extracting a subsequence if necessary, we
may assume that ψ(unk ) < +∞, and thus unk ∈ W 1,∞ (I), for every k. For simplicity and using
Fact 5.40, fix for each nk the continuous representative of unk , which we denote by the same name.
Then each unk is Mk -Lipschitz with Mk → M .
   Since unk → u in L2 (I), it is standard that we may extract a subsequence that converges to u
pointwise almost everywhere. Denote this further subsequence again by (unk )k , and let N ⊂ I be
a measure zero set such that unk → u pointwise in I \ N . Then for each x ̸= y in I \ N , we have
               |u(x) − u(y)| = lim |unk (x) − unk (y)| ≤ lim Mk |x − y| = M |x − y| .
                                k→∞                           k→∞

By Lemma A.3, u is a. e. equal to an M -Lipschitz function, so ψ(u) ≤ M by Fact 5.40, as needed.

    Second, suppose ∂x u↓(a) < slopeu (a, b). We proceed similarly, but with points to the left of
a instead. Namely, it cannot be the case that ∂x u↓(x) < slopeu (a, b) for all x ∈ (0, a), since by
continuity this would imply that ∂x u↓(0) ≤ slopeu (a, b) < 0, a contradiction. Hence we may define

                             a∗ := sup {x ∈ (0, a) : ∂x u↓(x) ≥ slopeu (a, b)} .

By continuity, we conclude that ∂x u↓(a∗ ) = slopeu (a, b), which also implies that a∗ < a. We claim
that a∗ is left-favourable. Indeed, if it was not, then for some δ > 0 we would have z(x) > 0 for
all x ∈ (a∗ − δ, a∗ + δ), and since z = ∂x ∂x u↓ a. e. we would conclude that ∂x u↓(x) > ∂x u↓(a∗ ) =
slopeu (a, b) for all x ∈ (a∗ , a∗ +δ), contradicting the choice of a∗ . We now claim that a∗ also satisfies
u(a∗ ) > u(b) and |slopeu (a∗ , b)| ≥ |slopeu (a, b)|.
    To prove the first inequality, we first observe that ∂x u↓(x) < slopeu (a, b) < 0 for all x ∈ (a∗ , a),
which by Lemma 5.25 implies that u↑ is constant in (a∗ , a). Since u↑ is continuous (because u and
u↓ are), we conclude that u↑(a∗ ) = u↑(a). Therefore we have

                                   u(a∗ ) − u(a) = u↓(a∗ ) − u↓(a) ≥ 0


                                                        41
since u↓ is nonincreasing, and hence u(a∗ ) ≥ u(a) > u(b). As for the second inequality, we have
                                                          
       (b − a∗ )(b − a) |slopeu (a∗ , b)| − |slopeu (a, b)|
                                  u(a∗ ) − u(b) u(a) − u(b)
                                                               
                       ∗
             = (b − a )(b − a)                     −
                                        b − a∗           b−a
                                                         
             = u(a ) − u(b) (b − a) − u(a) − u(b) (b − a∗ )
                     ∗

                                     Z                           !
                                                                                      
             = u↑(a) + u↓(a) −                 ∂x u↓(x) dx − u(b) (b − a) − u(a) − u(b) (b − a∗ )
                                       (a∗ ,a) | {z }
                                            <slopeu (a,b)
                                                  !

                                   b−a

             = u(a) − u(b) (b − a) − (b − a∗ ) + (a − a∗ )
             = 0.

Therefore in any case we have found a left-favourable a∗ ∈ (0, b) such that u(a∗ ) > u(b) and
|slopeu (a∗ , b)| ≥ |slopeu (a, b)|.
    Repeating an analogous argument for the right endpoint of the interval (a∗ , b), we find a right-
favourable b∗ ∈ (a∗ , 1) such that u(a∗ ) > u(b∗ ) and |slopeu (a∗ , b∗ )| ≥ |slopeu (a∗ , b)|, which concludes
the proof as explained above.

Proposition 5.44 (ψ-monotonicity of solutions). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the
solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then for all 0 ≤ t1 ≤ t2 < +∞, we have
ψ(u(t1 )) ≥ ψ(u(t2 )).

Proof. As in the proof of Proposition 5.38, this follows from Lemmas 5.37 and 5.43.

Corollary 5.45 (Preservation of Lipschitz regularity). Suppose u0 ∈ W 1,∞ (I), and let u ∈
C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then u(t) ∈
W 1,∞ (I) for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 5.44 and the definition of ψ.

5.7    Nonexpansiveness and order preservation
Our goal in this section is to establish that the semigroup Pt is nonexpansive and order preserving.
These properties will help us show that applying Pt to line restrictions in the multidimensional
setting behaves as expected by “making progress” toward monotonicity with each application.
    In the context of PDEs, one desirable way to show that a property is preserved through time is to
differentiate in time, and then pass the derivative inside the integral to exploit the definition of the
PDE. However, we need to establish some technical results before we can justify such calculations.
    We start with the following lemma, slightly adapted from [CT80], which reveals a close connec-
tion between order preservation and nonexpansiveness (here, in the supremum norm) of operators.
Hence our strategy will be to establish order preservation, and conclude nonexpansiveness.

Lemma 5.46. Let (Ω, Σ, µ) be a finite measure space, and let 1 ≤ p ≤ +∞. Let L∞ (Ω) ⊂ C ⊂
Lp (Ω) have the property that for all f ∈ C and r ∈ R, f + r ∈ C. Let T : C → Lp (Ω) be continuous


                                                       42
as a map from Lp (Ω) to Lp (Ω) (the continuity requirement may be dropped if p = ∞) satisfying

                                      T (f + r) = T (f ) + r a. e.                                  (22)

for every f ∈ C and r ∈ R. Then the following are equivalent:
 (a) For all f, g ∈ C, if f ≤ g a. e. then T f ≤ T g a. e. .

 (b) For all f, g ∈ C, (T f − T g)+ ≤ ess sup(f − g)+ a. e. .

 (c) For all f, g ∈ C, |T f − T g| ≤ ess sup|f − g| a. e. .

Proof. We show (a) =⇒ (b) =⇒ (c) =⇒ (a). First, assume (a) holds, and let f, g ∈ C. Suppose
r := ess sup(f − g)+ < +∞, since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Note that g + r ≥ f and
g + r ≥ g both a. e. . Thus by (a) and (22), we have

                     T g + (T f − T g)+ = T f ∨ T g ≤ T (g + r) = T g + r a. e. ,

so (T f − T g)+ ≤ r a. e. as needed. Next, suppose (b) holds, and let f, g ∈ C. Let r1 := ess sup(f −
g)+ and r2 := ess sup(g − f )+ ; note that ess sup|f − g| = max{r1 , r2 }, so we may assume that
r1 , r2 < +∞, since otherwise there is nothing to show. Then indeed, using (b),

          |T f − T g| = max (T f − T g)+ , (T g − T f )+ ≤ max{r1 , r2 } = ess sup|f − g| a. e. ,
                            

as needed. Finally, suppose (c) holds, and let f, g ∈ C satisfy f ≤ g a. e. . First, suppose
f, g ∈ L∞ (Ω). Let r := ess sup(g − f ), so that 0 ≤ r < +∞ by assumption. Then, by (c) and (22),

             ess sup{T f − T g + r} = ess sup{T (f + r) − T g} ≤ ess sup|T (f + r) − T g|
                                    ≤ ess sup|f − g + r| ≤ r ,

so T f ≤ T g a. e. as needed.
    Now, for general f, g ∈ C with f ≤ g a. e. , we may approximate them by sequences (fn )n , (gn )n
in L∞ (Ω) ⊂ C such that fn → f and gn → g in Lp (Ω) and moreover fn ≤ gn a. e. for each n;
indeed, letting fn := max{−n, min{n, f }} and likewise for gn , it is immediate that fn , gn ∈ L∞ (Ω)
with fn ≤ gn a. e. , and the convergences fn → f and gn → g in Lp (Ω) follow from the dominated
convergence theorem:     since |f − fn |p → 0 pointwise and |f − fn | ≤ |f | pointwise for each n, we
have limn→∞ Ω |fn − f |p dµ = 0.
              R

    The previous case yields T fn ≤ T gn a. e. for each n, and we claim that the continuity of T
implies that T f ≤ T g a. e. as well. Indeed, we have T fn → T f an T gn → T g in Lp (Ω) and thus

          (T f − T g)+ − (T fn − T gn )+ Lp (Ω) ≤ ∥(T f − T g) − (T fn − T gn )∥Lp (Ω)
                                                ≤ ∥T f − T fn ∥Lp (Ω) + ∥T g − T gn ∥Lp (Ω) → 0 ,

which means that (T fn − T gn )+ → (T f − T g)+ in Lp (Ω). But since (T fn − T gn )+ = 0 a. e. and
hence in Lp (Ω) for each n, we conclude that (T f − T g)+ = 0 a. e. , i. e. T f ≤ T g a. e. as needed.

    The following lemma is a specific formulation of the well-known Leibniz rule for differentiating
under the integral sign. It is a specialization of the version stated in [Che], and can be proved by
a standard argument using the Fubini-Tonelli theorem.

Lemma 5.47 (Differentiating under the integral sign). Let (Ω, Σ, µ) be a measure space and let
[a, b] ⊂ R. Let f : [a, b] × Ω → R be a jointly measurable function satisfying the following:

                                                   43
  1. f (t, ·) ∈ L1 (Ω) for a. e. t ∈ (a, b).

  2. f (·, x) is AC for a. e. x ∈ Ω. (Its a. e. defined partial derivative is denoted by ∂t f as usual.)

  3. It holds that                          Z         Z
                                                              |∂t f (t, x)| dµ dt < +∞ .
                                             (a,b)        Ω
                         R
Then the function t 7→       Ω f (t, x) dµ is AC and
                             Z                    Z
                        ∂t        f (t, x) dµ =       ∂t f (t, x) dµ          for a. e. t ∈ (a, b).
                              Ω                   Ω

   The following fact is a standard consequence of the definition of absolute continuity:

Fact 5.48. Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. If f, g : J → R are AC, then f ∨ g is AC.

Fact 5.49 (See e. g. [EG15, Theorem 4.4]). Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. Let 1 ≤ p < ∞ and
let f ∈ W 1,p (J). Then f + ∈ W 1,p (J) and ∂x (f + ) = χ{f >0} ∂x f a. e. in J.

    We also use the following standard formulation of a chain rule for Sobolev functions. This
version follows e. g. from [EG15, Theorem 4.4], which is stated for globally Lipschitz functions F ,
by using the fact that the image of f on J is bounded, so that the local Lipschitz condition suffices
(e.g. extend F linearly outside the image of f to obtain a C 1 (R), globally Lipschitz function F̃ ).

Fact 5.50. Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. Let 1 ≤ p < ∞, let f ∈ W 1,p (J), and let F ∈ C 1 (R)
be locally Lipschitz. Then F ◦ f ∈ W 1,p (J) and ∂x (F ◦ f )(x) = F ′ (f (x))∂x f (x) for a. e. x ∈ J.

   The following lemma essentially says that, given an element of a Bochner space, we can get a
handle on a concrete jointly measurable function “representing” that element in a precise sense.
This makes concrete the intuitive expectation that a solution u to our PDEs, which maps each
point in time to an element of L2 (I), should also give us a specific value at each point in time and
space “u(t, x)”.

Lemma 5.51. [DS58, Theorem 17, p. 198] Let (S, ΣS , µ) and (T, ΣT , λ) be measure spaces which
are either both finite or both positive and σ-finite, and let (R, ΣR , ρ) be their product. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞
and let F be a µ-integrable function on S to Lp (T, ΣT , λ, X ) where X is a real or complex Banach
space. Then there is a ρ-measurable function f on R to X , which is uniquely determined except
for a set of ρ-measure zero, such that f (s, ·) = F (s)Rfor µ-almost all s in S. Moreover f (·, t) is
µ-integrable Ron S for λ-almost all t and the integral S f (s, t)µ(ds), as a function of t is equal to
the element S F (s)µ(ds) of Lp (T, ΣT , λ, X ).

Corollary 5.52. Let [a, b] ⊂ R be a compact interval endowed with the Lebesgue measure, and let
F ∈ L1 (a, b; L2 (I)). Then there exists a jointly measurable function f ∗ : [a, b] × I → R satisfying

  1. f ∗ (s, ·) = F (s) in L2 (I) for a. e. s ∈ (a, b).

  2. f ∗ (·, x) ∈ L1 (a, b) for all x ∈ I.

Corollary 5.56 (Pt is order preserving). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I), and suppose u ≤ v a. e. . Then Pt u ≤ Pt v
a. e. for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Corollary 5.55.

Lemma 5.57. Every nondecreasing u ∈ L2 (I) is a stationary point of Pt , i. e. Pt u = u for all t.

Proof. By definition, u = u↑ + u↓ with u↑ = u and u↓ = 0, so E − (u) = 0. Thus for every v ∈ L2 (I),
                                  E − (v) ≥ 0 = E − (u) + ⟨0, v − u⟩ ,
so 0 ∈ ∂E − (u). Thus u(t) = u is the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state u.

Observation 5.58. Corollary 5.56 in particular implies that if u ≥ a a. e. for some a ∈ R, then
Pt u ≥ Pt a = a a. e. for all t > 0, the equality by Lemma 5.57.

   We also observe below that Pt is degree one positively homogeneous and additive when one
argument is a constant function, as the following results show.

Lemma 5.59. Let u ∈ U, β ∈ R, and v := u + β. Then v ∈ U with v↑ = u↑ + β and v↓ = u↓, and
thus E − (v) = E − (u).

Proof. This is a straightforward consequence of the definition of v↑, v↓.

Lemma 5.60. Let u ∈ U, α > 0, and v := αu. Then v ∈ U with v↑ = αu↑ and v↓ = αu↓, and
thus E − (v) = α2 E − (u).

Proof. This is a straightforward consequence of the definitions of v↑, v↓ and E − .

Lemma 5.61. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), β ∈ R, and v := u + β. Then ∂E − (u) = ∂E − (v).

Proof. By symmetry, it suffices to prove that ∂E − (u) ⊆ ∂E − (v). Let z ∈ ∂E − (u). Then for all
w ∈ U, using Lemma 5.59 twice and the definition of subdifferential,
               E − (w) = E − (w − β) ≥ E − (u) + ⟨z, w − β − u⟩ = E − (v) + ⟨z, w − v⟩ ,
and hence z ∈ ∂E − (v) as desired.

Lemma 5.62. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), α > 0, and v := αu. Then ∂E − (v) = α∂E − (u).

Proof. By symmetry, it suffices to prove that α∂E − (u) ⊆ ∂E − (v). Let z ∈ ∂E − (u); we claim that
αz ∈ ∂E − (v). Indeed for all w ∈ U, using Lemma 5.60 twice and the definition of subdifferential,
             E − (w) = α2 E − α1 w ≥ α2 E − (u) + z, α1 w − u = E − (v) + ⟨αz, w − v⟩ ,
                                                            

and hence αz ∈ ∂E − (v) as desired.

                                                  48
Proposition 5.63 (Effect of certain affine transformations on Pt ). Let u ∈ L2 (I), α > 0, and
β ∈ R. Then Pt (αu + β) = αPt u + β.

Proof. We suffices to prove the statement for u ∈ U; the general case then follows by approximating
U by H 1 (I) functions and the continuity of Pt from L2 (I) to L2 (I). For v := u + β, we observe that
v(t) := u(t) + β with v ′ (t) := u′ (t) is a solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state v,
since for each t > 0 for which −u′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (u(t)), Lemma 5.61 implies that −v ′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (v(t)).
    Similarly, let w := αu. Then w(t) := αu(t) with w′ (t) := αu′ (t) is the solution to the gradient
flow problem with initial state w, since for each t > 0 for which −u′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (u(t)), Lemma 5.62
implies that −w′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (w).

5.8   Convergence to monotone equilibrium
Since the directed Dirichlet energy E − (Pt u) decays over time and Pt u converges to some limit as
t → ∞ (by Proposition 5.24), we expect this limit to be a monotone function. Let us establish this
fact and other properties of that limit.

Lemma 5.64. Let u ∈ U satisfy E − (u) = 0. Then u is nondecreasing.

Proof. Since E − (u) = 0, we have ∂x u↓ = 0 a. e. in I. Hence u↓ is a constant function, while u↑ is
nondecreasing by definition.

Proposition 5.65. Let u ∈ L2 (I). Then there exists a nondecreasing u∗ ∈ U, unique as an element
of L2 (I), such that Pt u → u∗ in L2 (I) as t → ∞.

Proof. Since E − achieves its minimum (namely 0, on e. g. constant functions) and Pt u ∈ D(∂E − )
for all t > 0, [AC84, Theorem 2, p. 160] implies that there exists a minimizer u∗ of E − such that
Pt u ⇀ u∗ weakly in L2 (I). This means that u∗ ∈ U with E − (u∗ ) = 0, so by Lemma 5.64 u∗ is
nondecreasing. By Proposition 5.24 (which we may apply because, fixing any t0 > 0, we have
Pt0 u ∈ D(∂E − ) ⊆ D(E − ) = U), Pt u also converges strongly in L2 (I), and it is standard that the
weak and strong limits agree and that this limit is unique.

   Therefore the following definition is justified:

Definition 5.66 (Monotone equilibrium). Let P∞ : L2 (I) → U be the operator mapping each
u ∈ L2 (I) to the unique (as an element of L2 (I)) nondecreasing u∗ ∈ U such that Pt u → u∗ in
L2 (I) as t → ∞. We call P∞ u the monotone equilibrium of u.

   We now pass to the limit P∞ some useful properties of Pt .

Proposition 5.67 (“Directed nonexpansiveness” of P∞ ). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). Then
                       Z                         Z
                                        + 2
                                                              2
                                                     (u − v)+ dx .
                                                 
                           (P∞ u − P∞ v)    dx ≤
                             I                           I

Proof. This follows by a standard limit argument as follows. Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). By Corollary 5.55,
∥(Pt u − Pt v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(u − v)+ ∥L2 (I) for all t > 0. By definition of P∞ , we have Pt u → P∞ u and
Pt v → P∞ v in L2 (I) as t → ∞. Using the triangle inequality, we obtain
       ∥(Pt u − Pt v)+ − (P∞ u − P∞ v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(Pt u − Pt v) − (P∞ u − P∞ v)∥L2 (I)
                                                ≤ ∥Pt u − P∞ u∥L( I) + ∥Pt v − P∞ v∥L2 (I) → 0 ,


                                                  49
so (Pt u−Pt v)+ → (P∞ u−P∞ v)+ in L2 (I) as t → ∞. Since ∥(Pt u−Pt v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(u−v)+ ∥L2 (I) for
all t > 0, we conclude that ∥(P∞ u − P∞ v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(u − v)+ ∥L2 (I) , which gives the conclusion.

Corollary 5.68 (P∞ is order preserving). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I), and suppose u ≤ v a. e. . Then
P∞ u ≤ P∞ v a. e. .

Proof. This is a direct consequence of Proposition 5.67.

Proposition 5.69. P∞ is nonexpansive, and therefore continuous, as an L2 (I) → L2 (I) map.

Proof. Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). In the proof of Proposition 5.67, we showed that ∥(Pt u − Pt v) − (P∞ u −
P∞ v)∥L2 (I) → 0, which implies that ∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) → ∥P∞ u − P∞ v∥L2 (I) . But ∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) ≤
∥u − v∥L2 (I) for all t > 0 since (Pt )t≥0 is a nonexpansive semigroup, so the conclusion follows.

Lemma 5.70. For every nondecreasing u ∈ L2 (I), we have P∞ u = u.

Proof. This follows from Lemma 5.57 along with the convergence Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I).

    Strong convergence to the monotone equilibrium, together with the preservation of regularity
results from Section 5.5, allows us to obtain regularity of the monotone equilibrium as well:

Proposition 5.71 (H 1 regularity of the monotone equilibrium). Let u ∈ H 1 (I). Then P∞ u ∈
H 1 (I) with φ(P∞ u) ≤ φ(u).

Proof. Recall the functional φ from Section 5.5. Since u ∈ H 1 (I), φ(u) < +∞. By Proposition 5.38,
we conclude that φ(Pt u) ≤ φ(u) < +∞ for all t > 0. Since φ is lower semicontinuous by Claim 5.33
and Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I) by Proposition 5.24, we conclude that φ(P∞ u) ≤ φ(u) < +∞. Hence
P∞ u ∈ H 1 (I).

Proposition 5.72 (Lipschitz regularity of the monotone equilibrium). Let u ∈ W 1,∞ (I). Then
P∞ u ∈ W 1,∞ (I) with ψ(P∞ u) ≤ ψ(u).

Proof. Recall the functional ψ from Section 5.6. Since u ∈ W 1,∞ (I), ψ(u) < +∞. By Proposi-
tion 5.44, we conclude that ψ(Pt u) ≤ ψ(u) < +∞ for all t > 0. Since ψ is lower semicontinuous by
Claim 5.41 and Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I) by Proposition 5.24, we conclude that ψ(P∞ u) ≤ ψ(u) < +∞.
Hence P∞ u ∈ W 1,∞ (I).

   We observe that P∞ also behaves nicely under the appropriate class of affine transformations.

Proposition 5.73 (Effect of certain affine transformations on P∞ ). Let u ∈ L2 (I), α > 0, and
β ∈ R. Then P∞ (αu + β) = αP∞ u + β.

Proof. Since Pt v → P∞ v as t → ∞ for each v ∈ L2 (I), applying Proposition 5.63 yields
                                                             h       i
       P∞ (αu + β) = lim Pt (αu + β) = lim [αPt u + β] = α lim Pt u + β = αP∞ u + β .
                        t→∞                 t→∞                    t→∞

   We can finally conclude, via Lemma 5.46, that P∞ is nonexpansive in the L∞ norm.

Proposition 5.74 (P∞ is nonexpansive in L∞ norm). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). Then |P∞ u − P∞ v| ≤
ess sup|u − v| a. e. in I.



                                                   50
Proof. We verify the conditions of Lemma 5.46 with (Ω, Σ, µ) the set I endowed with the Lebesgue
measure, p = 2, C = L2 (I), and T = P∞ . It is clear that for all f ∈ L2 (I) and r ∈ R, f + r ∈ L2 (I).
By Proposition 5.69, P∞ is a continuous L2 (I) → L2 (I) map. Moreover, for all f ∈ L2 (I) and
r ∈ R, the condition P∞ (f + r) = P∞ f + r holds by Proposition 5.73. Finally, for all f, g ∈ L2 (I)
with f ≤ g a. e. , we have that P∞ f ≤ P∞ g a. e. by Corollary 5.68. The conclusion follows.


6     Directed transport-energy inequality
In this section, we establish a connection between the PDE studied above and the Wasserstein
distance between the initial state u and its monotone equilibrium P∞ u, via the dynamical approach
embodied by the Benamou-Brenier formula, and then tensorize this result from one dimension to
[0, 1]d . For optimal transport concepts, we follow the presentation and formalism of [San15], and
also refer to [AGS05; Vil09].
     This section is organized as follows. After preliminary considerations in Section 6.1, Section 6.2
shows that the PDE results from Section 5 imply a result about optimal transport (Wasserstein)
distance, namely a one-dimensional transport-energy inequality, thereby implementing the ideas
described in Section 1.2.3 of the proof overview. The idea is to show that our solution to the
directed heat equation
                                              ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u
satisfies the so-called continuity equation

                                              ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0 ,

which prescribes that “particles” (distributed according to measure ϱt ) move according to the
velocity field vt ; in our setting, we have dϱt = u dx and vt = − ∂xuu↓ (Proposition 6.8), so that
indeed ∂x (ϱt vt ) = −∂x ∂x u↓ = −∂t ϱt . For functions u bounded close to 1, we can informally
disregard the denominator in vt and imagine, for simplicity, that vt ≈ −∂x u↓. Then thanks to the
exponential decay of                              Z
                                         −      1
                                       E (u) =      (∂x u↓)2 dx
                                                2
from the previous section, we conclude that the right-hand side of the Benamou-Brenier formula
                              Z 1                                                             
             W22 (µ, ν) = min      ∥vt ∥2L2 (ϱt ) dt : ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0, ϱ0 = µ, ϱ1 = ν
                                      0

from Proposition 6.6 is roughly upper bounded by E − (u), where (informally) the measures dµ =
u dx and dν = (P∞ u) dx correspond to the initial state and monotone equilibrium of the directed
heat equation, as probability measures9. Therefore we conclude in Theorem 6.11 the transport-
energy inequality in one dimension,

                                             W22 (µ, µ∞ ) ≲ E − (u) .

Finally, the continuity equation also lets us conclude natural and useful properties of solutions of
the directed heat equation, such as mass conservation (Corollaries 6.9 and 6.10) and the property
that solutions can only ever lose mass in any given prefix of the interval [0, 1] (Corollary 6.15).
   9
     More formally, we decompose the evolution from time t = 0 to time t → ∞ into intervals of unit time length, but
by the exponential energy decay, this summation is dominated by the initial term E − (u).



                                                         51
    The next goal is to establish the tensorized (multidimensional) transport-energy inequality,
thereby implementing the ideas described in Section 1.2.4 of the proof overview. In Section 6.3, we
develop the definitions and technical tools required to show our “Pythagorean composition” result
(Lemma 6.34), which yields a recipe for proving multidimensional transport-energy inequalities
(with respect to the “directed Wasserstein distance”) by induction (Lemma 6.35).
    Finally, Section 6.4 ties together the tensorization recipe described above with the one-dimensional
results specific to our PDE from the previous sections. We show that, by taking an initial state
f : [0, 1]d → R with corresponding measure dµ = f dx, applying the directed heat semigroup one co-
ordinate at a time, and putting together the one-dimensional transport-energy inequalities at each
of these steps via properties of our PDE such as order preservation and nonexpansiveness (which
enable the induction to go through), we indeed obtain a monotone final state f ∗ : [0, 1]d → R with
corresponding measure dµ∗ = f ∗ dx for which the multidimensional transport-energy inequality
applies (Theorem 6.51):                             Z
                                                                      2
                                   W22 (µ → µ∗ ) ≲             ∇− f       dx .
                                                      [0,1]d


6.1    Preliminaries for optimal transport
We start by introducing notation and definitions relevant to the theory of optimal transport.

Projections. Say X × Y is a product space and z = (x, y) is an element in this space. Then the
projection operator onto the first coordinate, denoted interchangeably by π1 or πx depending on
the context, is given by π1 (z) = πx (z) = x. Similarly, π2 (z) = πy (z) = y. We extend this definition
in the natural way to projections from larger product spaces onto smaller product spaces. For
example, given the product space X1 × · · · × Xd and index set I ⊂ [d], I = {i1 , . . . , in }, the operator
πI projects any element in this space down to an element of Xi1 × · · · × Xin . We also use the
shorthand π−I := π[d]\I .
    Now, let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set and let I ⊆ [d]. For a point x ∈ Ω, we write the projections
xI := πI (x) and x−I := π−I (x), and we also write x = (xI , x−I ). Then, for the set Ω, we write the
projections ΩI := {xI : x ∈ Ω} and Ω−I := {x−I : x ∈ Ω}. For small sets I or [d] \ I, we also use
shorthand notation such as xi := x{i} and Ω−i−j := Ω−{i,j} .

Pushforward measure. Given measurable spaces (X, ΣX ) and (Y, ΣY ), measure µ on X, and
measurable map T : X → Y , the pushforward measure T# µ on Y is the measure satisfying

                    (T# µ)(B) = µ(T −1 (B))                      for all B ∈ ΣY , or equivalently,
          Z                      Z
               ϕ(y) d(T# µ)(y) =   ϕ(T (x)) dµ(x)                 for all measurable ϕ : Y → R.
           Y                       X

    For a measure γ on product space X × Y , we say that (π1 )# γ and (π2 )# γ, which are measures
on X and Y respectively, are the first and second marginals of γ, respectively.
    If (Ω3 , Σ3 ) is another measurable space and S : Y → Z a measurable map, then it holds that
(S ◦ T )# µ = S# (T# µ).

Transport plans. For two probability spaces (Ω1 , Σ1 , µ1 ) and (Ω2 , Σ2 , µ2 ), we write Π(µ1 , µ2 )
for the set of couplings, or transport plans, between µ1 and µ2 , namely probability measures γ on
the product space Ω1 × Ω2 whose first and second marginals are µ1 and µ2 , respectively.



                                                     52
Space of probability measures. For any Borel set Ω ⊂ Rd , let P (Ω) denote the space of all
(Borel) probability measures on Ω. We endow P (Ω) with the weak topology, which is the topology
of weak convergence with respect to bounded
                                          R    continuous
                                                      R functionals. Namely, we say µn converges
weakly to µ in P (Ω), and write µn ⇀ µ, if Ω ϕ dµn → Ω ϕ dµ for all bounded continuous ϕ : Ω → R.

Wasserstein distances. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and let p ∈ [1, +∞). Let µ, ν ∈
P (Ω). Given transport plan γ ∈ Π(µ, ν), we define the cost Cp (γ) by
                                            Z                        1/p
                                                                  p
                                Cp (γ) :=             |x − y| dγ(x, y)     .
                                                Ω×Ω

We then define the p-Wasserstein distance between µ and ν by

                                      Wp (µ, ν) :=          inf       Cp (γ) ,
                                                       γ∈Π(µ,ν)

and we also often refer to the quantity Wpp (µ, ν) := Wp (µ, ν)p . It is standard that Wp (·, ·) is a
distance metric on P (Ω).

Fact 6.1 (Wasserstein distance metrizes weak convergence; see e. g. [San15, Theorems 5.10 and 5.11]).
Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set, and let p ∈ [1, ∞). Then for a sequence (µn )n in P (Ω) and
µ ∈ P (Ω), we have µn ⇀ µ if and only if Wp (µn , µ) → 0.

Corollary 6.2. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set, and let p ∈ [1, ∞). Then for sequences (µn )n
and (νn )n in P (Ω) with µn ⇀ µ and νn ⇀ ν for µ, ν ∈ P (Ω), we have Wp (µn , νn ) → Wp (µ, ν). In
other words, the p-Wasserstein distance is continuous in (the weak topology on) P (Ω).

Proof. By the triangle inequality, we have

      Wp (µ, ν) − Wp (µ, µn ) − Wp (νn , ν) ≤ Wp (µn , νn ) ≤ Wp (µn , µ) + Wp (µ, ν) + Wp (ν, νn ) .

By Fact 6.1, the LHS and RHS converge to Wp (µ, ν) as n → ∞, so the conclusion follows.

6.2   Optimal transport via Benamou-Brenier
Let {ϱt : t ∈ [0, T ]} be a family of measures on I and {vt : t ∈ [0, T ]} be a family of velocity fields
such that vt ∈ L1 (ϱt ) for each t. We start by defining what it means for the family (ϱt , vt ) to solve
the continuity equation
                                          ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0 .

Definition 6.3 (Weak solution; see [San15, Section 4.1.2]). Let (ϱt , vt ) be a family of mea-
sure/velocity field pairs indexed by t ∈ [0, T ] such that vt ∈ L1 (ϱ) for each t. We say that (ϱt , vt )
is a Rweak solution to the continuity equation if, for every test function ψ ∈ C 1 (I), the function
t 7→ I ψ dϱt is absolutely continuous in t and, for a. e. t, we have
                                       Z            Z
                                    ∂t ψ dϱt = (∂x ψ)vt dϱt .                                     (24)
                                            I            I

In this case, we call ϱ0 and ϱT the initial and final states of the solution, respectively (this makes
sense because the above implies that t 7→ ϱt is continuous for the weak convergence of measures).

Remark 6.4. We will only work with absolutely continuous measures dϱt = u dx, u ∈ L2 (I).

                                                       53
Remark 6.5. Applying (24) with a constant test function ψ shows that every solution to the
continuity equation is mass conserving. In particular, Proposition 6.8 below implies that this is
true of the semigroup Pt ; see Corollary 6.9.

   The continuity equation is connected to Wasserstein distance via the Benamou-Brenier formula:

Proposition 6.6 (Benamou-Brenier formula; see e. g. [San15, Theorem 5.28]). Let µ, ν be proba-
bility measures on I. Then
                              Z 1                                                            
             W22 (µ, ν) = min      ∥vt ∥2L2 (ϱt ) dt : ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0, ϱ0 = µ, ϱ1 = ν ,
                                   0

where the constraint above means that (ϱt , vt )t∈[0,1] is a weak solution to the continuity equation
with initial state µ and final state ν.

    We now show our solution to the directed heat equation yields a solution to the continuity equa-
tion. This allows us to conclude several nice properties of this solution, such as mass conservation,
as well as apply the Benamou-Brenier formula to obtain a transport-energy inequality.

Condition 6.7. u ∈ U is a. e. positive and bounded away from zero, and satisfies I u dx = 1.

numerator is even pointwise well-defined.)

Corollary 6.10 (P∞ is mass conserving). Let u ∈ L2 (I). Then I P∞ u dx = I u dx.

   We now upper bound the Wasserstein distance between u ∈ U and its monotone equilibrium
by combining the Benamou-Brenier formula with the exponential decay of the directed Dirichlet
energy, at least as long as u is positive and bounded away from zero.

Theorem 6.11 (Transport-energy inequality in one dimension). There exists a constantR C > 0 such
that the following holds. Let u ∈ U be positive, bounded away from zero, and satisfy (0,1) u dx = 1.
Define the measures dµ := u dx and dµ∞ := (P∞ u) dx. Then

                                                             C −

                                                           inf u

    The definition of the velocity field vt in Proposition 6.8, along with the fact that ∂x u(t)↓ ≤ 0,
gives vt ≥ 0, which intuitively says that “particles only move to the right”. This suggests that the
solution u(t) can only lose mass in any prefix of the interval (0, 1) over time. The following items
make this observation rigorous.

Definition 6.14. Let µ and ν be probability measures on R. We say µ dominates ν, and write
µ ⪰ ν, if for every x ∈ R we have µ(−∞, x) ≥ ν(−∞, x).

Corollary 6.15. Let u ∈ U satisfy Condition 6.7. Define the measures dµ := u dx and dµ∞ :=
(P∞ u) dx. Then µ ⪰ µ∞ .

Proof. We may view µ and µ∞ as absolutely continuous measures on all of R, taking zero outside
[0, 1]. Now, for each x ∈ [0, 1], Corollary 6.13 gives
                                    Z             Z
                       µ(−∞, x) =          u dy ≥      (P∞ u) dy = µ∞ (−∞, x) .
                                       (0,x)          (0,x)

On the other hand, for x < 0 both sides are zero, and for x > 1, both sides are 1 by Corollary 6.10.


6.3    Directed optimal transport and Pythagorean composition
We now introduce directed versions of basic optimal transport concepts and theory, with the goal of
combining a directed version of weak Kantorovich duality with a multidimensional directed version
of the transport-energy inequality from Theorem 6.11 to obtain our desired directed Poincaré
inequality via a perturbation argument. In the interest of space, we refrain starting a systematic
study of directed optimal transport, but rather limit ourselves to the results we require.

Definition 6.16 (Quasimetric). Let X be a set. An extended real-valued function d : X × X →
[0, +∞], not necessarily symmetric, is called a quasimetric on X if it satisfies the following:

   1. For all x, y ∈ X, d(x, y) ≥ 0 with d(x, y) = 0 if and only if x = y.

   2. For all x, y, z ∈ X, d(x, z) ≤ d(x, y) + d(y, z).

Definition 6.17. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set, and let µ and ν be probability measures on Ω. We
define the set Π(µ → ν) of directed couplings, or directed transport plans, from µ to ν as
                                                 Z                          
                    Π(µ → ν) := γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) :            χ{x̸⪯y} dγ(x, y) = 0 ,
                                                           Ω×Ω

where the integral is well-defined because the set {(x, y) ∈ Rd × Rd : x ̸⪯ y} is open and hence Borel
measurable. Note that the condition could also be written as γ ({x ̸⪯ y}) = 0, or γ ({x ⪯ y}) = 1.

                                                      57
Definition 6.18 (Directed Wasserstein distance). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and let
p ∈ [1, ∞). Given two probability distributions µ, ν over Ω, we define the directed p-Wasserstein
distance from µ to ν by
                                 Wp (µ → ν) :=     inf   Cp (γ) ,
                                                   γ∈Π(µ→ν)

and we write Wpp (µ → ν) := Wp (µ → ν)p .     Note that we may have Wp (µ → ν) = +∞, and that
Wp (· → ·) is not symmetric in general.

Observation 6.19. For all measures µ and ν, we have Π(µ → ν) ⊂ Π(µ, ν) and hence Wp (µ, ν) ≤
Wp (µ → ν).

Lemma 6.20 (Gluing lemma; see e. g. [San15, Lemma 5.5]). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set. Let µ, ϱ, ν
be probability measures on Ω, and let γ + ∈ Π(µ, ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ, ν). Then there exists a probability
measure σ on Ω × Ω × Ω such that (πx,y )# σ = γ + and (πy,z )# σ = γ − .

   The proof of that Wp (· → ·) is a quasimetric follows the presentation of [San15, Lemma 5.4],
with a simple additional argument to handle directed couplings.

Lemma 6.21 (Composition of directed transport plans). In Lemma 6.20, if γ + ∈ Π(µ → ϱ) and
γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν), then (πx,z )# σ ∈ Π(µ → ν).

Proof. Let γ := (πx,z )# σ. First, since (πx )# γ = (πx ◦ πx,z )# σ = (πx ◦ πx,y )# σ = (πx )# γ + = µ,
and similarly (πz )# γ = ν, we have γ ∈ Π(µ, ν). Moreover, by definition of pushforward measure
we have
    Z                        Z                              Z
                                                                                          
          χ{x̸⪯z} dγ(x, z) =          χ{x̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z) ≤            χ{x̸⪯y} + χ{y̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z)
      Ω×Ω
                             ZΩ×Ω×Ω                         Z Ω×Ω×Ω
                           =          χ{x̸⪯y} dσ(x, y, z) +           χ{y̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z)
                              Ω×Ω×Ω                          Ω×Ω×Ω
                             Z                           Z
                           =       χ{x̸⪯y} dγ + (x, y) +       χ{y̸⪯z} dγ − (y, z) = 0 ,
                              Ω×Ω                        Ω×Ω

the last equality since γ + and γ − are directed couplings. Hence γ ∈ Π(µ → ν) as claimed.

Proposition 6.22. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and p ∈ [1, ∞). Then Wp (· → ·) is a
quasimetric on P (Ω).

Proof. It is clear that Wp (µ → ν) ≥ 0 always. If µ = ν then the identity coupling shows that
Wp (µ → ν) = 0, and conversely if Wp (µ → ν) = 0 then, by Observation 6.19, Wp (µ, ν) = 0 and
hence µ = ν. It remains to show that Wp (· → ·) satisfies the triangle inequality.
   Let µ, ϱ, ν be probability measures on Ω. Let γ + ∈ Π(µ → ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν). Then since
we also have γ + ∈ Π(µ, ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ, ν), apply Lemma 6.20 to obtain a probability measure σ
on Ω×Ω×Ω such that (πx,y )# σ = γ + and (πy,z )# σ = γ − . Let γ := (πx,z )# σ, so that γ ∈ Π(µ → ν)
by Lemma 6.21. Then
                     Z                      1/p Z                               1/p
      Wp (µ → ν) ≤          |x − z|p dγ(x, z)    =            |x − z|p dσ(x, y, z)
                        Ω×Ω                              Ω×Ω×Ω
                  = ∥|x − z|∥Lp (σ) ≤ ∥|x − y| + |y − z|∥Lp (σ) ≤ ∥|x − y|∥Lp (σ) + ∥|y − z|∥Lp (σ)
                    Z                            1/p Z                                1/p
                                      p                                     p
                  =           |x − y| dσ(x, y, z)      +             |y − z| dσ(x, y, z)
                        Ω×Ω×Ω                                  Ω×Ω×Ω

                      Z                          1/p       Z                         1/p

                  =           |x − y| dγ (x, y)          +          |y − z| dγ (y, z)          .
                        Ω×Ω                                   Ω×Ω

Since γ + ∈ Π(µ → ϱ), γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν) were arbitrary, Wp (µ → ν) ≤ Wp (µ → ϱ) + Wp (ϱ → ν).

    The directed Wasserstein distance arises naturally in the one-dimensional case when one prob-
ability measure dominates the other in the sense of Definition 6.14, as we now show.

Proposition 6.23 (Specialization of [San15, Theorem 2.9]). Let p ∈ (1, +∞). Let µ and ν be two
absolutely continuous probability measures on I with strictly positive densities. Then there exists a
unique γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) attaining Cp (γ) = Wp (µ, ν), and in fact γ = (id, Tmon )# µ where Tmon : I → I
is a nondecreasing function that is a. e. uniquely determined, and id denotes the identity map.

Corollary 6.24. If in Proposition 6.23 we have µ ⪰ ν, then in fact γ ∈ Π(µ → ν). As a
consequence, Wp (µ → ν) = Wp (µ, ν).

Proof. We claim that Tmon (x) ≥ x for every x ∈ I. Indeed, suppose Tmon (x) < x for some x ∈ I.
Then
                                                          −1
             ν(0, Tmon (x)) = (Tmon# µ)(0, Tmon (x)) = µ(Tmon (0, Tmon (x))) ≥ µ(0, x) ,
                                           −1 (0, T
the inequality because certainly (0, x) ⊆ Tmon     mon (x)), but we do not rule out at this point that
T remains constant for a while after x. But since Tmon (x) < x and ν has strictly positive density
by assumption, we conclude that

                                  ν(0, x) > ν(0, Tmon (x)) ≥ µ(0, x) ,

contradicting the assumption that µ ⪰ ν. Hence Tmon (x) ≥ x for every x ∈ I as claimed. We now
show that γ ∈ Π(µ → ν). Let S := {(x, y) ∈ I × I : x ≤ y}. Since γ = (id, Tmon )# µ, we have

                γ(S) = µ((id, Tmon )−1 (S)) = µ({x ∈ I : x ≤ Tmon (x)}) = µ(I) = 1 ,

so γ ∈ Π(µ → ν) as claimed.

    We now introduce the formal language for two related operations: constructing a probability
measure by its marginal and conditional distributions, and the inverse process of extracting marginal
and conditionals from a probability measure, which is also called disintegration. We refer the reader
to [AGS05, Section 5.3] for an overview of these ideas from a measure-theoretic perspective.
    We use the following definition from [AGS05]. For X and Y separable metric spaces and
x ∈ X 7→ µx ∈ P (Y ) a measure-valued map, we say µx is a Borel map if x 7→ µx (B) is a Borel map
for any Borel set B ⊂ Y , or equivalently if this holds for any open set A ⊂ Y . In this case we also
have that                                    Z
                                       x 7→     f (x, y) dµx (y)
                                                 Y
is Borel for any bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function f : X × Y → R.

Definition 6.25 (Construction and disintegration). Let S be a finite set and let I ⊂ S. Let
xS\I ∈ RS\I 7→ µ|xS\I ∈ P (RI ) be a Borel map (the conditionals). Then for any bounded (or
nonnegative) Borel function f : RS → R, the RS\I → R map
                                        Z

                                            RI

is Borel. Therefore, for any µS\I ∈ P (RS\I ) (the marginal ), we define µ ∈ P (RS ) implicitly by
                      Z          Z                   Z

                       RS           RS\I                    RI

and we formally write µ =     RS\I µ|xS\I dµS\I .
    Conversely, given µ ∈ P (RS ), we let µS\I := (πS\I )# µ ∈ P (RS\I ) and write (µ|xS\I )xS\I ∈RS\I ⊂
P   I
R (R ) for the µS\I -a. e. uniquely determined Borel family of probability measures such that µ =
 RS\I µ|xS\I dµS\I , and we call this decomposition a disintegration of µ.


Examples and simplified notational conventions. Since the index notation in the definition
above can be somewhat laborious to parse, let us briefly give two concrete settings we will use
below, and introduce simplified notational conventions for those settings. The simplifications are
supposed to be mnemonic for what we have already defined for projections.

   1. For a probability measure µ ∈ P (Rd ) and index i ∈ [d], we may disintegrate µ into a
      marginal
          R     on index set [d] \ {i} and conditionals supported along i-th coordinate,
                                                                                  R          and write

   2. For a transport plan γ ∈ P (Rd × Rd ), we write the index set as [2d] = S = Sx ∪ Sy for
      Sx := [d] and Sy := [2d] \ [d], so we write each element in the support of γ as a tuple (x, y)
      with x ∈ RSx and y ∈ RSy . Then, given index set I ⊂ [d], we may disintegrate γ into a
      marginal on index set SM := (Sx \ Ix ) ∪ (Sy \ Iy ) for Ix := I and Iy := {d + i : i ∈ I},
      so that we think of the marginal as determining all but the I-indexed coordinates of the
      points x and y; and we view      the conditionals as supported along the Ix ∪ Iy directions.
      This disintegration is γ = RSM γ|xS dγSM . To be clear, here we have γSM ∈ P (RSM ) and
                                  R

    For absolutely continuous probability measures on [0, 1]d , the following more familiar charac-

density functions are u−i : [0, 1]d−i → [0, +∞) and u|x−i : [0, 1] → [0, +∞) for each x−i ∈ [0, 1]d−i ,

                                                     60
Proof. First, note that it is standard that u−i is a Borel map since it is the integral of the section
u(x−i , ·) at each point x−i . It is hence immediate that each u|x−i is also Borel. Thus µ−i and all
µ|x−i are indeed absolutely continuous probability measures.
    We now check the conditions of Definition 6.25. We claim that the map x−i ∈ Rd−i 7→ µ|x−i ∈
P (R) is Borel. Let B ⊂ R be a Borel set; we may assume that B ⊂ [0, 1] without loss of generality,
since all measures assign zero outside this interval. Then the map x−i ∈ Rd−i 7→ µ|x−i (B) takes

This is again, at each point, the integral of a section over the subspace B, so it is standard that

    It remains to verify (26). Let f : Rd → [0, +∞) be a nonnegative Borel function (and the result

Definition 6.27 (Aligned transport plans). RLet I ⊊ [d] be nonempty and let µ, ν ∈ P (Rd ). Let

Note that this condition is well-defined because the set being measured is open and hence Borel.
By convention, we say that every γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) is [d]-aligned. For any nonempty I ⊆ [d], we denote
the set of I-aligned transport plans by ΠI (µ, ν). When we have a singleton I = {i}, we write i
directly in the place of I in this definition.

Definition 6.28. For nonempty I ⊆ [d] and µ, ν ∈ P (Rd ), write ΠI (µ → ν) := Π(µ → ν)∩ΠI (µ, ν).

    The following results let us find aligned transport plans by transporting mass only within i-
aligned lines.

Lemma 6.31 (Measurable selection; specialization of [Vil09, Corollary 5.22]). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a
bounded set. Let p ∈ [1, ∞). Let A be a measurable space and let a 7→ (µa , νa ) be a measurable
function A → P (R)×P (R) with each µa and νa having bounded support. Then there is a measurable
choice a 7→ γa such that, for each a ∈ A, γa ∈ Π(µa , νa ) and Cp (γa ) = Wp (µa , νa ).

    Now, we show that the directed, aligned 2-Wasserstein distance enjoys a nice “Pythagorean”
composition property: composing I-aligned and J-aligned directed transport plans, for I and J
disjoint, yields an I ∪ J-aligned directed transport plan whose cost is obtained from the costs of
the two other plans via the Pythagorean theorem. Arguments of this nature are well-known in the
undirected case, e. g. a similar statement appears in [Vil09, p. 572].

y−J ̸= z−J . Indeed,

6.4    From one-dimensional PDE to optimal transport in the cube
We now tie most of the foregoing theory together by showing how to inductively transform a
function on the unit cube into a monotone function, one coordinate at a time via the directed heat
semigroup machinery, keeping track of the cost using the directed optimal transport machinery.
    For this part of the proof, it is convenient to restrict our attention to Lipschitz functions. As we
will see, the Lipschitz property of f : [0, 1]d → R is preserved under taking the monotone equilibrium
along each axis-aligned direction, which enables an inductive argument over the coordinates.
    Denote the set of Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R by Lip (the dimension d will always be
clear from context).

Observation 6.36. A function f : [0, 1]d → R is Lipschitz if and only if its restriction to every
axis-aligned line is uniformly Lipschitz, i. e. there exists some M > 0 such that, for every i ∈ [d]
and x−i ∈ [0, 1]d−i , f (x−i , ·) : [0, 1] → R is M -Lipschitz.

Definition 6.37 (i- and I-monotonicity). Let i ∈ [d]. We say f : [0, 1]d → R is i-monotone if
its every restriction along direction i is nondecreasing, i. e. if for every x−i ∈ [0, 1]d−i , f (x−i , ·) is
nondecreasing. For I ⊆ [d], we say f is I-monotone if it is i-monotone for every i ∈ I. We write
MonI for the set of I-monotone functions.

Observation 6.38. Mon[d] is simply the set of monotone functions on [0, 1]d .

   The following useful characterization of i-monotone functions in terms of k-aligned lines follows
by definition:

Observation 6.39. Let i, k ∈ [d] be distinct. Then for all f : [0, 1]d → R, f is i-monotone if and
only if the following holds: for every z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k , and for all xi ≤ yi in [0, 1], f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤
f (z−i−k , yi , ·).

Definition 6.40 (Monotone equilibrium operator). Let k ∈ [d]. We define the operator Mk :
Lip → ([0, 1]d → R) as follows: for each function f ∈ Lip, x−k ∈ [0, 1]d−k and xk ∈ [0, 1], the function

                                                            66
Mk f : [0, 1]d → R satisfies

                                     (Mk f )(x−k , xk ) := (P∞ f (x−k , ·))(xk ) ,

where the application of P∞ is well-defined because each f (x−k , ·) is Lipschitz and hence in L2 (I).

    The following lemma shows that Mk preserves Lipschitzness, confirming that Mk f is well-
defined pointwise as a real-valued function, as opposed to only defined up to sets of measure zero.

Lemma 6.41. Let k ∈ [d]. Let f ∈ Lip. Then Mk f ∈ Lip.

Proof. Let M > 0 be such that every axis-aligned line restriction of f is M -Lipschitz. We claim
that every axis-aligned line restriction of Mk f is M -Lipschitz. We first check the k-aligned lines.
For any x−k ∈ [0, 1]d−k Proposition 5.72 implies that

                        ψ((Mk f )(x−k , ·)) = ψ(P∞ f (x−k , ·)) ≤ ψ(f (x−k , ·)) ≤ M ,

the last inequality and the conclusion that (Mk f )(x−k , ·) is M -Lipschitz by Fact 5.40.
   It remains to check the other line restrictions. Let i ∈ [d] \ {k} and let z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k .
Let xi ̸= yi ∈ [0, 1]. Since the axis-aligned line restrictions of f (in particular, in direction i) are
M -Lipschitz, we have

                           sup |f (z−i−k , xi , wk ) − f (z−i−k , yi , wk )| ≤ M |xi − yi | .
                         wk ∈[0,1]

Proposition 5.74 implies that

 |P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) − P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·)| ≤ ess sup|f (z−i−k , xi , wk ) − f (z−i−k , yi , wk )| ≤ M |xi − yi |
                                                   wk ∈[0,1]
                                                                                                       (29)
a.e. in I, and since P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) and P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·) are (Lipschitz) continuous as observed
above, we conclude that (29) holds pointwise. Since (29) holds for every i ∈ [d] \ {k} and z−i−k ∈
[0, 1]d−i−k , and since

         |(Mk f )(z−i−k , xi , ·) − (Mk f )(z−i−k , yi , ·)| = |P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) − P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·)|

pointwise, we get that every axis-aligned line restriction of Mk f is M -Lipschitz, and Mk f ∈ Lip.

Lemma 6.42 (Mk application makes progress). Let k ∈ [d], and let I ⊆ [d] \ {k}. Let f ∈
Lip ∩ MonI . Then Mk f ∈ Lip ∩ MonI∪{k} .

Proof. The fact that Mk f ∈ Lip is given by Lemma 6.41, and the fact that Mk f is k-monotone
follows from the definition of Mk and the fact that the monotone equilibrium P∞ f (x−k , ·) is
nondecreasing. Now, let i ∈ I, so that f is i-monotone. By Observation 6.39, we have that
for every z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k , and for all xi ≤ yi in [0, 1], f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤ f (z−i−k , yi , ·). By
Corollary 5.68, we obtain that P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤ P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·) a. e. in I, and since these two
functions are (Lipschitz) continuous, this inequality holds pointwise. Thus (Mk f )(z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤
(Mk f )(z−i−k , yi , ·), and again by Observation 6.39, Mk f is i-monotone. This concludes the proof.


Lemma 6.43 (Mk preserves bounds). Let f ∈ Lip. Let a ≤ b be real numbers and suppose
a ≤ f ≤ b. Then a ≤ Mk f ≤ b.

                                                           67
Proof. This follows by the definition of Mk together with Corollary 5.68 and Lemma 5.70.

Definition 6.47 (Coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium). For each f ∈ Lip, define f ∗ ∈ Lip, the
coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium of f , by

                                                        f ∗ := Md Md−1 · · · M1 f .

   Note that indeed f ∗ is indeed Lipschitz by Lemma 6.41, and it is monotone:

Proposition 6.48. Let f ∈ Lip. Then f ∗ is monotone.

Proof. This follows by repeatedly applying Lemma 6.42, and recalling that Mon[d] is the set of
monotone functions on [0, 1]d .


                                                                    69
   By Proposition 5.73 and the definition of Mk , we also observe that the coordinate-wise mono-
tone equilibrium behaves nicely with respect to certain affine transformations:

Observation 6.49. Let f ∈ Lip. Let α > 0 and β ∈ R. Then (αf + β)∗ = αf ∗ + β.

   Similarly, Lemma 6.44 implies that taking the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium is a non-
expansive operation:

Observation 6.50. Let f, g ∈ Lip. Then ∥f ∗ − g ∗ ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) ≤ ∥f − g∥L2 ((0,1)d ) . Since 0∗ = 0
by Lemma 5.70 (where we write 0 for the constant zero function), we conclude in particular that
∥f ∗ ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) ≤ ∥f ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) .

   We are now ready to combine the ingredients from the previous sections and establish the
transport-energy inequality. Note that this inequality is most effective when the function f is
pointwise bounded close to 1.

the following holds. Let a ∈ (0, 1), and let f ∈ Lip satisfy 1 − a ≤ f ≤ 1 + a and [0,1]d f dx = 1.

                                                                         71
7     Directed Poincaré from Wasserstein and Kantorovich
In this section, we establish our directed Poincaré inequality (Theorem 1.1) by combining the
transport-energy inequality from Theorem 6.51 with a directed version of (weak) Kantorovich
duality via a perturbation argument, thereby implementing the ideas from Section 1.2.5 of the proof
overview. The general theme of going between transport inequalities and Poincaré inequalities, as
well as the spirit of the technical arguments presented here, are well-known in the undirected case;
we refer the reader to [Vil09, Chapters 7 and 22] for a comprehensive presentation and primary
references, and to [Liu20] for an undirected version of the precise reduction we instantiate here
(that is, from a transport-energy inequality to a Poincaré inequality).
    For simplicity, we do not attempt to initiate a systematic study of duality in directed optimal
transport, or to state results in the broadest possible generality, but rather limit ourselves to what
is required for the aforementioned goal. In Section 7.1, we introduce the notion of directed weak
Kantorovich duality and establish some of its properties. The main results are Proposition 7.4,
which gives a lower bound on the directed Wasserstein distance between two measures in terms
the so-called (directed) Hamilton-Jacobi operator H   ⃗ (recall that the previous section gave an upper
bound on this distance via the transport-energy inequality), and Proposition 7.6, which relates the
action H⃗ t h(x) of this operator back to the directed gradient ∇+ h(x) (which, recall, we wish to have
in the right-hand side of our Poincaré inequality). Then, in Section 7.2, we apply these tools via
a careful choice of dual function (a “perturbation argument”) to conclude the directed Poincaré
inequality, first for continuously differentiable functions (Theorem 7.8) and then, via a standard
approximation argument, for all H 1 functions (Theorem 1.1).

7.1    Directed weak Kantorovich duality and Hamilton-Jacobi operator
Kantorovich duality plays a fundamental role in the study of Wasserstein distance. The weak part
of this duality allows one to lower bound the Wasserstein distance between two distributions by
means of “test functions” satisfying a certain constraint. Here, we translate a small part of this
rich theory to the directed setting.

Lemma 7.1 (Weak duality). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and let µ, ν ∈ P (Ω). Suppose
ϕ ∈ L1 (ν) and ψ ∈ L1 (µ) satisfy

                              ϕ(y) − ψ(x) ≤ |x − y|2       ∀x ⪯ y in Ω .

Then                                              Z            Z
                                  W22 (µ → ν) ≥       ϕ dν −       ψ dµ .
                                                  Ω            Ω

Proof. Suppose γ ∈ Π(µ → ν). Then
                 Z

                 ZΩ×Ω

                 ZΩ×Ω
               =      (ϕ(y) − ψ(x)) dγ(x, y)                                (Since γ ∈ Π(µ → ν))

Since this holds for every γ ∈ Π(µ → ν), the result follows.

   This duality results motivates the following definition. Let Cb (Ω) denote the set of bounded
continuous functions Ω → R.

7.2    Perturbation argument
We can now obtain the main result, first for C 1 ([0, 1]d ) functions and then for all of H 1 ((0, 1)d ) by an
approximation argument. The key idea of the perturbation argument is to use the same (mean zero)
function h in two different roles: 1) to construct absolutely continuous probability measures from
h and its coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium, namely dµ = (1 + th) dx and dµ∗ = (1 + th∗ ) dx,
for small t > 0; and 2) in the “test function” −th for weak duality on W22 (µ → µ∗ ) via the directed
Hamilton-Jacobi operator.

Ω := (0, 1)d , and let h ∈ C 1 (Ω). Then

where h∗ is the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium of h.
